Enumeration of certain subsets of uprooted trees and spherical parking functions
Nayana Shibu Deepthi, Chanchal Kumar, Gargi Lather

Abstract
. Spherical G G -parking functions are a distinguished subset of standard monomials, arising from the skeleton ideals of the G G -parking function ideal. Explicit enumeration formulas for spherical G G -parking functions are known only for a few classes of graphs. In this paper, we consider a family of graphs G ℓ G_{\ell} ( 1 ≤ ℓ ≤ n − 2 1\leq\ell\leq n-2 ), obtained from the complete graph K n + 1 K_{n+1} by deleting the ℓ \ell edges joining vertex 1 1 to the vertices in F ℓ = { n − ℓ + 1 , … , n } F_{\ell}=\{n-\ell+1,\ldots,n\} . The uprooted spanning trees of G ℓ − { 0 } G_{\ell}-\{0\} correspond to the set 𝒰 n 1 ≁ F ℓ \mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}} of uprooted trees with vertex set [ n ] [n] in which vertex 1 1 is not adjacent to any vertex in F ℓ F_{\ell} , and we establish that | 𝒰 n 1 ≁ F ℓ | = ( n − 1 ) n − ℓ − 2 ​ ( n − 2 ) ℓ ​ ( n − ℓ − 1 ) |\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-2)^{\ell}(n-\ell-1) . We derive this formula combinatorially and independently recover it as an application of the matrix tree theorem, obtaining some combinatorial identities as consequences. Finally, we determine the number of spherical G ℓ G_{\ell} -parking functions as | sPF ⁡ ( G ℓ ) | = ( n − 1 ) n − 3 ​ ( n − ℓ − 1 ) 2 |\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})|=(n-1)^{n-3}(n-\ell-1)^{2} .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary: 05C30, 05C05, 05C50; Secondary: 05E40

1.
Introduction

Parking functions, introduced by Konheim and Weiss
[
13
]
in the context of computer storage allocation, have since become central objects in enumerative and algebraic combinatorics.
Recall that a sequence
(p_{1},\ldots,p_{n})\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
is a (
classical
)
parking function of length
n
if its non-decreasing rearrangement
p_{i_{1}}\leq\cdots\leq p_{i_{n}}
satisfies
p_{i_{j}}<j
for all
1\leq j\leq n
.
The number of parking functions of length
n
is
(n+1)^{n-1}
.
A clever proof of this formula, based on a circular street argument, was given by Pollak
[
20
]
.
By Cayley’s formula,
(n+1)^{n-1}
is also the number of labeled trees with
n+1
vertices.
This equality in cardinality led to many bijective constructions.
Kreweras
[
14
]
gave a recursively defined bijection, and Stanley
[
21
]
formulated the problem of finding a non-recursive bijective proof.
Further, several solutions have been found, one of which can be found in
[
11
]
.

Parking functions have many generalizations.
An interesting generalization was given by Postnikov and Shapiro
[
19
]
in 2004.
Let
G
be a connected graph with vertex set
V(G)=\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and designated root
0
.
Let
A(G)=[a_{ij}]_{0\leq i,j\leq n}
denote the adjacency matrix of
G
, where
a_{ij}
denote the number of edges between vertices
i
and
j
.
We write
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
for the set of non-root vertices, and define
d_{A}(i)=\sum_{j\in V(G)\setminus A}a_{ij}
, to be the number of edges from
i
to vertices outside
A
, for
i\in A\subseteq[n]
.
A function
\mathcal{P}\colon[n]\to\mathbb{N}
is called a
G
-parking function
(with respect to root
0
) if, for every non-empty subset
A\subseteq[n]
, there exists
i\in A
such that
\mathcal{P}(i)<d_{A}(i)
.
The set of all
G
-parking functions is denoted
\mathrm{PF}(G)
.
Further, Postnikov and Shapiro
[
19
]
associated the
G
-parking function ideal

\mathcal{M}_{G}\;=\;\biggl\langle m_{A}=\prod_{i\in A}x_{i}^{d_{A}(i)}\;:\;\emptyset\neq A\subseteq[n]\biggr\rangle\;\subseteq\;R=\mathbb{K}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}],

to the graph
G
and showed that its standard monomials correspond bijectively to the
G
-parking functions.
When
G
is the complete graph
K_{n+1}
, the
G
-parking functions reduce precisely to the classical parking functions of length
n
.

Chebikin and Pylyavskyy
[
5
]
constructed bijections between
G
-parking functions and spanning trees of
G
.
Later, Perkinson, Yang, and Yu
[
18
]
introduced a
Depth-First-Search
variant of Dhar’s burning algorithm
[
7
]
to produce an explicit bijection
\phi\colon\mathrm{PF}(G)\to\mathrm{SPT}(G)
between
G
-parking functions and the set of all spanning trees
\mathrm{SPT}(G)
of
G
.
Gaydarov and Hopkins
[
10
]
extended this bijection to multigraphs.

Motivated by the combinatorics of chip-firing on graphs, Dochtermann
[
9
]
introduced the following subideals of
\mathcal{M}_{G}
.
For
0\leq k\leq n-1
, the
k
-skeleton ideal
of
G
is

\mathcal{M}_{G}^{(k)}\;=\;\bigl\langle m_{A}\;:\;\emptyset\neq A\subseteq[n],\;\left\lvert A\right\rvert\leq k+1\bigr\rangle\;\subseteq\;\mathcal{M}_{G}.

The standard monomials of
\mathcal{M}_{G}^{(k)}
form a family of sets that changes with the value of
k
.
In particular,
\mathcal{M}_{G}^{(n-1)}=\mathcal{M}_{G}
, and the standard monomials coincide with the
G
-parking functions.
The filtration
\mathcal{M}_{G}^{(n-2)}\subset\mathcal{M}_{G}
naturally determines the class of
spherical
G
-parking functions
introduced in
[
9
]
.
A function
\mathcal{P}\colon[n]\to\mathbb{N}
is called a
spherical
G
-parking function
if

\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}=\prod_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{\mathcal{P}(i)}\in\mathcal{M}_{G}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{G}^{(n-2)}.

We denote the set of spherical
G
-parking functions by
\mathrm{sPF}(G)
.
For further studies on skeleton ideals and skeletal generalizations of parking functions, see
[
15
,
2
]
.
While the enumeration of spherical
G
-parking functions is known for certain families of graphs, explicit formulas are available only in a few cases.

Throughout this paper, unless stated otherwise, we consider only finite simple vertex-labeled graphs
G=(V(G),E(G))
, with vertex set
V(G)=\{0\}\cup[n]
and designated root
0
.
If there exists an edge between
u
and
v
in
G
, then we say that
u
and
v
are
adjacent
to each other and write
u\sim v
, and we denote this edge by
e_{u,v}
.
If there is no edge between them, then we say that
u
and
v
are
not adjacent
to each other and write
u\nsim v
.
Let
G-\{v\}
denote the graph obtained from
G
by deleting the vertex
v\in V(G)
along with all edges incident to it.
For
E^{\prime}\subseteq E(G)
, let
G-E^{\prime}
denote the graph obtained from
G
by deleting all edges in
E^{\prime}
.
In particular, we write
G-\{e_{u,v}\}
when
E^{\prime}=\{e_{u,v}\}
is a single edge.
A
rooted tree
is a tree with a distinguished vertex called the
root
, where each edge is directed away from the root, defining a natural orientation for the graph.
For a rooted tree
T
, we use
\operatorname{son}_{T}(v)
and
\operatorname{par}_{T}(u)
to denote the set of sons of a vertex
v\in V(T)
and the parent of a non-root vertex
u\in V(T)
in
T
, respectively.
For any undefined terms
and notations in this article, see
[
1
,
3
]
.

An
uprooted
(
spanning
)
tree
is a rooted (spanning) tree in which the root is strictly larger than all of its children.
We denote the set of all uprooted spanning trees of a graph
G
by
\mathcal{U}(G)
.
In particular, let
\mathcal{U}_{n}
denote the set of all uprooted trees with vertex set
[n]
.
It is known that
|\mathcal{U}_{n}|=(n-1)^{n-1}
(see
[
4
,
6
]
).
Dochtermann conjectured the existence of a natural bijection between
\mathrm{sPF}(K_{n+1})
and
\mathcal{U}_{n}
.
This was proved by Kumar, Lather, and Sonica
[
16
]
using a modified Depth-First-Search burning algorithm, and independently by Dochtermann and King
[
8
]
(using a Breadth–First–Search variant of Dhar’s burning algorithm
[
7
]
).
Thus,
|\mathrm{sPF}(K_{n+1})|=|\mathcal{U}_{n}|=(n-1)^{n-1}
.
Moreover, adding or deleting edges between the root
0
and the other vertices of a graph does not affect the number of spherical parking functions
[
16
]
.
Since vertex
1
is the smallest non-root vertex, it is natural to study how deleting edges incident to vertex
1
affects the enumeration of spherical parking functions.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\nsim n}
denote the set of all uprooted trees with vertex set
[n]
in which vertices
1
and
n
are not adjacent.
The cardinality of
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\nsim n}
is given by
|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\nsim n}|=(n-1)^{n-3}~(n-2)^{2}
(see
[
16
,
6
]
).
For graph
G=K_{n+1}-\{e_{1,n}\}
, Kumar et al.
[
16
]
constructed a bijection between
\mathrm{sPF}(G)
and
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\nsim n}
, thereby enumerating
\mathrm{sPF}(G)
.
In this paper, we continue this line of investigation.
We consider graphs obtained from
K_{n+1}
by deleting additional sets of edges, and derive explicit formulas for
|\mathrm{sPF}(G)|
for these families.

The paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we introduce the graph
G_{\ell}
and, using the bijection of Chauve, Dulucq, and Guibert
[
4
]
between
\mathcal{U}_{n}
and a certain subset of labeled rooted trees with vertex set
[n]
, we enumerate the uprooted spanning trees of
G_{\ell}-\{0\}
.
In Section
3
, we recover this enumeration as an application of the matrix tree theorem and obtain several combinatorial identities as consequences.
In Section
, we recall the necessary background on spherical
G_{\ell}
-parking functions and outline our approach to their enumeration.
Finally, in Section
, we determine
|\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})|
.

2.
Enumeration of uprooted trees in
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}

Our aim is to study the spherical parking functions of a family of graphs obtained by deleting certain edges from
K_{n+1}
.
For integers
n\geq 3
and
1\leq\ell\leq n-2
, we define

F_{\ell}=\{n-\ell+1,\,n-\ell+2,\ldots,n\}\;\subseteq\;[n],

and let
G_{\ell}
be the graph with vertex set
[n]\cup\{0\}
and root
0
, obtained from
K_{n+1}
by removing the
\ell
edges joining vertex
1
to each vertex in
F_{\ell}
. That is,

G_{\ell}=K_{n+1}-\{e_{1,v}\ \colon v\in F_{\ell}\}.

We write
G_{\ell}^{\prime}=G_{\ell}-\{0\}
for the induced subgraph of
G_{\ell}
with vertex set
[n]
.
Let
\mathcal{U}_{n}
denote the set of all uprooted trees with vertex set
[n]
, and for
n\geq 3
and
1\leq\ell\leq n-2
, we define the subset

\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}=\{T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}\ \colon 1\nsim v\text{ for any }v\in F_{\ell}\}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{n}.

The set of uprooted spanning trees of
G_{\ell}^{\prime}
is precisely
\mathcal{U}(G_{\ell}^{\prime})=\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.

One of the key results of this section is a closed formula for the cardinality of
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.

Theorem 2.1
.

For
n\geq 3
and
1\leq\ell\leq n-2
,

|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}~(n-2)^{\ell}~(n-\ell-1).

For
\ell=1
, our formula recovers the count
(n-1)^{n-3}(n-2)^{2}
, obtained in
[
16
]
.
Our proof of Theorem
2.1
is based on the bijection
\Psi\colon\mathcal{U}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}_{n}
of Chauve, Dulucq, and Guibert
[
4
]
, where
\mathcal{B}_{n}
denotes the set of labeled rooted trees with vertex set
[n]
in which vertex
1
is a non-root leaf.
We now recall the construction of
\Psi
from
[
4
]
(see also
[
16
]
).

Construction of
\Psi
.

Let
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
with root
r
. Since
T
is uprooted, we have
r\neq 1
. Consider the maximal increasing subtree
T_{0}
of
T
containing the vertex
1
.
Deleting the edges of
T_{0}
decomposes
T
into rooted subtrees
T_{1},\ldots,T_{k}
, each with at least one edge, and let
r_{i}
denote the root of
T_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.
The root
r
of
T
is the root of exactly one of these subtrees, say
T_{j}
, where
r_{j}=r
.
Since
1
is the root of
T_{0}
, it will be a leaf of
T_{j}
.

Suppose that
T_{0}
has
m
vertices. Then
T_{0}
can be encoded by an increasing tree
\overline{T}_{0}
with vertex set
[m]
together with the set
S_{0}
of labels appearing in
T_{0}
.
We write
T_{0}=(\overline{T}_{0},S_{0})
.
Let
\widetilde{S}_{0}=(S_{0}\setminus\{1\})\cup\{r\}
.
The pair
(\overline{T}_{0},\widetilde{S}_{0})
determines an increasing tree
\widetilde{T}_{0}
whose root is
r_{0}=\min(\widetilde{S}_{0})
.
We attach the subtree
T_{j}
to
\widetilde{T}_{0}
at vertex
r
, and then attach each of the remaining subtrees
T_{i}
(for
i\neq j
) at the vertex
r_{i}
.
The resulting rooted tree
T^{\prime}=\Psi(T)
has root
r_{0}
and vertex
1
as a non-root leaf.
Hence
\Psi(T)\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
.

Inverse construction.

We now describe
\Psi^{-1}\colon\mathcal{B}_{n}\to\mathcal{U}_{n}
.
Let
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
with root
r^{\prime}
.
Since vertex
1
is a non-root leaf of
T^{\prime}
, we have
r^{\prime}\neq 1
. Let
T^{\prime}_{0}
be the maximal increasing subtree of
T^{\prime}
rooted at
r^{\prime}
.
Deleting the edges of
T^{\prime}_{0}
decomposes
T^{\prime}
into rooted subtrees
T^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,T^{\prime}_{k}
, each containing at least one edge, and let
r^{\prime}_{i}
denote the root of
T^{\prime}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq k
.
Let
T^{\prime}_{j}
be the unique subtree containing the vertex
1
as a leaf.

Replace the label
r^{\prime}_{j}
in
T^{\prime}_{0}
by
1
, and relabel the vertices so that their relative order is preserved.
This produces an increasing subtree
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}_{0}
rooted at
1
.
Attach
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}_{0}
to
T^{\prime}_{j}
at the vertex
1
, and then attach each of the remaining subtrees
T^{\prime}_{i}
(for
i\neq j
) at its root
r^{\prime}_{i}
.
The resulting rooted tree
T
belongs to
\mathcal{U}_{n}
, and by construction satisfies
\Psi(T)=T^{\prime}
.

As noted earlier,
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
is a (proper) subset of
\mathcal{U}_{n}
.
To enumerate
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
, we study its image under the bijection
\Psi\colon\mathcal{U}_{n}\longrightarrow\mathcal{B}_{n}
.
In this section, we show that
\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
can be decomposed into explicitly described disjoint subsets of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
, and we determine the cardinality of each subset.
We begin with a classical enumeration result.

Theorem 2.2

(
[
17
]
*Theorem 3.3)
.

Let
f(n,k)
denote the number of forests with
n
labeled vertices consisting of
k
disjoint trees, where
k
specified vertices belongs to distinct components. Then, for
1\leq k\leq n
, we have
f(n,k)=k\ n^{n-k-1}.

For
n\geq 3
and
1\leq\ell\leq n-2
, we define

\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}=\{T\in\mathcal{B}_{n}\colon 1\nsim v\text{ for any }v\in F_{\ell}\}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n}.

Let
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, and let
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
be the unique tree such that
T^{\prime}=\Psi(T)
. From the construction of
\Psi
, the leaf
1
is adjacent to a vertex
j
in
T^{\prime}
if and only if
j=\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)
.
Hence,

\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}.

To describe this image more precisely, we introduce two disjoint subsets of
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}
as follows.

\mathbf{B}=\{T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}\colon\operatorname{root}(T^{\prime})=r^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}\text{ and }r^{\prime}\nsim j,\text{ for all }j>r^{\prime}\},

and

\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\{T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}\colon\operatorname{root}(T^{\prime})=r^{\prime}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}\text{ and }r^{\prime}\nsim v,\text{ for all }v\in F_{\ell}\}.

We then have the following.

Lemma 2.3
.

Let
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
be the subsets of
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}
defined above.
Then we have

\mathbf{B}\ \coprod\ \mathbf{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}).

Proof.

Recall that for each
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
, by the bijection
\Psi
, there exists a unique
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
such that
T^{\prime}=\Psi(T)
.
Let
r
and
r^{\prime}
denote the roots of
T
and
T^{\prime}
, respectively.
Further, we have seen that
\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}
.
By construction, the sets
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
are disjoint, i.e.,
\mathbf{B}\cap\mathbf{B}^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
We now show that each of these sets is contained in
\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
.

Let
T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}
.
Then
T^{\prime}
is a tree in
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}
whose root
r^{\prime}
lies in
F_{\ell}
, and for all
v\in\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}}{(r^{\prime})}
, we have
v<r^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
, and we have
\mathbf{B}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.
Moreover, the maximal increasing subtree
T^{\prime}_{0}
of
T^{\prime}
with root
r^{\prime}
is precisely
\{r^{\prime}\}
.
By the construction of
\Psi
, the maximal increasing subtree
T_{0}
of
T
rooted at
1
is
\{1\}
.
Thus, in this case,
T^{\prime}=\Psi(T)=T
.
This shows that
\mathbf{B}\subseteq\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
, and
\Psi(T)=T
for all
T\in\mathbf{B}
.

Now, let
T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
.
Then
r^{\prime}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
, and by definition,
r^{\prime}
is not adjacent to any vertex in
F_{\ell}
.
Since the leaf
1
in
T^{\prime}
is also not adjacent to any vertex in
F_{\ell}
, it follows that in the corresponding tree
T
, the parent
\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\notin F_{\ell}
.
We next show that no vertex in
F_{\ell}
can be a son of the vertex
1
in the tree
T
.
Consider the maximal increasing subtree
T^{\prime}_{0}
of
T^{\prime}
rooted at
r^{\prime}
, and let
\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}_{0}}(r^{\prime})
denote the set of sons of
r^{\prime}
in
T^{\prime}_{0}
, with
|\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}_{0}}(r^{\prime})|=s
.
In the construction of the inverse of
\Psi
, let
r_{j}^{\prime}
denote the root of the subtree
T^{\prime}_{j}
of
T^{\prime}
containing the leaf
1
.
Suppose first that
r_{j}^{\prime}=r^{\prime}
.
Then the maximal increasing subtree
T_{0}
of
T
rooted at
1
is obtained from
T^{\prime}_{0}
by relabeling
r^{\prime}
with
1
, while keeping all other vertex labels unchanged.
Hence, in this case,
\operatorname{son}_{T_{0}}{(1)}=\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}_{0}}{(r^{\prime})}
, and therefore none of the vertices in
F_{\ell}
is a son of vertex
1
in
T
.
Now suppose
r_{j}^{\prime}\neq r^{\prime}
.
Then we have
r_{j}^{\prime}>r^{\prime}
, and
T_{0}
is obtained by replacing the label
r_{j}^{\prime}
by
1
in
T^{\prime}_{0}
and relabeling the vertices so that their relative order is preserved.
Accordingly, the least labeled vertex in
\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}_{0}}{(r^{\prime})}
will be relabeled as
r^{\prime}
.
Regardless of whether
r_{j}^{\prime}
belongs to
\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}_{0}}{(r^{\prime})}
or not, since
r^{\prime}\notin F_{\ell}
and
r^{\prime}\nsim v
for all
v\in F_{\ell}
, we have that at least
s
vertices in
(\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}_{0}}{(r^{\prime})}\setminus\{r_{j}^{\prime}\})\cup\{r^{\prime}\}
are not in
F_{\ell}
.
It then follows from the construction that
\operatorname{son}_{T_{0}}{(1)}\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
.
Hence, in both cases, none of the vertices in
F_{\ell}
can be either the parent or a son of vertex
1
in the tree
T
satisfying
\Psi(T)=T^{\prime}
.
Hence,
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
, and therefore
T^{\prime}\in\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
.
Combining this with the earlier part of the proof, we conclude that
\mathbf{B}\ \coprod\ \mathbf{B}^{\prime}\subseteq\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
.
∎

We have already observed that
\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}
, and that the sets
\mathbf{B}
and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
are disjoint subsets of
\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
.
To complete the description of
\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
, we now turn our attention to the remaining trees in
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}\setminus(\mathbf{B}\coprod\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
, and investigate those whose corresponding preimage under
\Psi
lies in
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}=\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})\setminus(\mathbf{B}\coprod\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
.
Then the trees in
\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}
are precisely those for which the root
r^{\prime}
belongs to
\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
and is adjacent to exactly one vertex in
F_{\ell}
; that is,

r^{\prime}\sim v\text{ for some }v\in F_{\ell},\quad\text{and}\quad r^{\prime}\nsim w\text{ for all }w\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}.

Moreover, there does not exist an increasing path

r^{\prime}\rightarrow b_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow b_{t}\rightarrow\gamma,\quad(t\geq 1),

with
b_{i}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}\setminus\{r^{\prime}\}
and
\gamma\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}
satisfying
\gamma<v
, such that either

•

1
is a descendant of
v
, or

•

1
is a descendant of a vertex
\rho>v
, where there exists an increasing path

r^{\prime}\rightarrow b_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow b_{t}\rightarrow\rho

with
b_{i}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}\setminus\{r^{\prime}\}
.

Proof.

Let
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}
.
Since
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}\subseteq\mathcal{B}_{n}
, there exists a unique
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}
such that
T^{\prime}=\Psi(T)
.
Let
r
and
r^{\prime}
denote the roots of
T
and
T^{\prime}
, respectively.
We now examine the trees
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}\setminus(\mathbf{B}\coprod\mathbf{B}^{\prime})
for which the corresponding
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.

If
r^{\prime}=n
, then
T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}
, that contradicts our assumption.
Now, suppose that
r^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{n\}
.
As
T^{\prime}\notin\mathbf{B}
, we have that
r^{\prime}
is adjacent to some
j\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{r^{\prime}\}
with
j>r^{\prime}
.
Considering the maximal increasing subtree
T^{\prime}_{0}
of
T^{\prime}
rooted at
r^{\prime}
, we observe that
V(T^{\prime}_{0})\subseteq F_{\ell}
, since
r^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{n\}
.
Therefore, under the inverse construction of
\Psi
, the vertex
r^{\prime}
is relabeled as
1
, and we obtain
\operatorname{son}_{T_{0}}(1)\subseteq F_{\ell}
in the maximal increasing subtree
T_{0}
of
T
.
Thus, the vertex
1
is adjacent to some vertices in
F_{\ell}
in
T
.
Hence
T\notin\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
, and in this case we have
T^{\prime}\notin\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
.

Let
r^{\prime}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
.
If the root
r^{\prime}
is not adjacent to any vertex in
F_{\ell}
, then
T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
.
Since we are considering
T^{\prime}\notin\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
, it follows that
r^{\prime}
must be adjacent to at least one vertex in
F_{\ell}
.
Suppose that
r^{\prime}
is adjacent to exactly
m
vertices (
m\geq 2
) in
F_{\ell}
, say
\{w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{m}\}\subseteq F_{\ell},\quad\text{with }w_{1}<w_{2}<\cdots<w_{m}
.
Consider the maximal increasing subtree
T_{0}^{\prime}
of
T^{\prime}
with root
r^{\prime}
.
Recall that
r_{j}^{\prime}
denotes the root of the subtree
T_{j}^{\prime}
, which belongs to the collection of subtrees obtained by deleting the edges of
T_{0}^{\prime}
from
T^{\prime}
, and that contains the leaf
1
.
We have that
r_{j}^{\prime}
is either equal to
r^{\prime}
or a non-root vertex of
T_{0}^{\prime}
.
Suppose first that
r_{j}^{\prime}=r^{\prime}
.
Then, by the inverse construction of
T
from
T^{\prime}
, the maximal increasing subtree
T_{0}
of
T
is obtained from
T_{0}^{\prime}
by relabeling
r^{\prime}
as
1
, while all other vertices retain their labels.
Therefore, in this case,
T\notin\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
, since in
T
the vertex
1
is adjacent to all the vertices in
\{w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{m}\}\subseteq F_{\ell}
.
Now, suppose that
r_{j}^{\prime}\neq r^{\prime}
.
Then, in the inverse construction of
T
from
T^{\prime}
, the maximal increasing subtree
T_{0}
of
T
rooted at
1
is obtained by replacing
r_{j}^{\prime}
with
1
in the label set of
T_{0}^{\prime}
and relabeling the vertices accordingly.
If
r_{j}^{\prime}<w_{1}
, then all vertices
w_{i}
remain unchanged during the relabeling, and hence in
T
we have
1\sim w_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
.
If
r_{j}^{\prime}\geq w_{m}
, then after relabeling, we observe that
1\sim w_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq m-1
.
Furthermore, if
w_{1}<\cdots<w_{t}\leq r_{j}^{\prime}<w_{t+1}<\cdots<w_{m}
, for some
1\leq t<m
, then in the subtree
T_{0}
obtained from
T_{0}^{\prime}
, the vertex
1
becomes adjacent to all the vertices in
\{w_{t+1},w_{t+2},\dots,w_{m}\}
.
Thus, in every case where
r_{j}^{\prime}\neq r^{\prime}
, the leaf
1
in
T
is adjacent to at least one vertex in
F_{\ell}
. Therefore,
T\notin\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
, and hence
T^{\prime}\notin\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
whenever the root
r^{\prime}
of
T^{\prime}
is adjacent to at least two vertices in
F_{\ell}
.

Now, let us consider the case where
r^{\prime}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
and
r^{\prime}\sim v
for some
v\in F_{\ell}
, but
r^{\prime}\nsim w
for any
w\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}
.
We claim that if
T^{\prime}
contains an increasing path of the form

(2.1)

r^{\prime}\rightarrow b_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow b_{t}\rightarrow\gamma,\quad(t\geq 1),

where
b_{i}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}\setminus\{r^{\prime}\}
and
\gamma\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}
with
\gamma<v
, then
T\notin\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.

Suppose that such increasing paths exist in
T^{\prime}
.
Without loss of generality, assume that
\gamma
is the minimal vertex smaller than
v
among all such paths in
T^{\prime}
.
Consider the maximal increasing subtree
T_{0}^{\prime}
of
T^{\prime}
rooted at
r^{\prime}
, where
1
is a descendant of some
r_{j}^{\prime}\in V(T_{0}^{\prime})
such that the path
r_{j}^{\prime}\rightarrow a_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow a_{k}\rightarrow 1
satisfies
a_{1}<r_{j}^{\prime}
.
Suppose first that
r_{j}^{\prime}=r^{\prime}
or
r_{j}^{\prime}\leq\gamma
.
Since
\gamma<v
and
r^{\prime}\sim v
, the inverse construction relabels
r^{\prime}
as
1
, while the vertex
v
retains its label.
Hence, in
T
, the vertex
1
is adjacent to a vertex in
F_{\ell}
, and therefore
T\notin\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.
If instead
r_{j}^{\prime}>\gamma
, then the inverse construction replaces
r^{\prime}
by
1
and relabels
v
with the largest element in
V(T_{0}^{\prime})
that is smaller than
v
.
Moreover, the existence of an increasing path of the form (
2.1
) ensures that the vertex
v
in
T_{0}^{\prime}
is relabeled by a value at least
\gamma
.
As a result, in
T
, the vertex
1
is adjacent to a vertex in
F_{\ell}
, and hence
T\notin\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.
Therefore, whenever an increasing path of the form (
2.1
) exists in
T^{\prime}
, we have
T^{\prime}\notin\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
.

Thus, let us now consider those trees
T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}\setminus(\mathbf{B}\coprod\mathbf{B}^{\prime})

for which
r^{\prime}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
and
r^{\prime}\sim v
for some
v\in F_{\ell}
, while
r^{\prime}\nsim w
for any
w\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}
, and moreover, no increasing path of the form (
2.1
) exists in
T^{\prime}
.
We now examine this situation by distinguishing two subcases depending on whether the vertex
1
is a descendant of
v
or not.

Case 1.

Suppose that
1
is a descendant of
v
.
In this case, within
T_{0}^{\prime}
, we necessarily have
r_{j}^{\prime}\geq v
.
Since there are no increasing paths of the form (
2.1
), the inverse construction relabels
v
with the largest vertex in
V(T_{0}^{\prime})\cap\{2,3,\ldots,n-\ell\}
.
It follows that, in
T
, the vertex
1
is not adjacent to any vertex in
F_{\ell}
, and therefore
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.

Case 2.

Suppose that
1
is not a descendant of
v
.
We consider the maximal increasing subtree
T_{0}^{\prime}
of
T^{\prime}
rooted at
r^{\prime}
.
There are two possibilities, depending on the position of
r_{j}^{\prime}
.
If
r_{j}^{\prime}=r^{\prime}
, then the maximal subtree
T_{0}
of
T
is obtained by replacing
r^{\prime}
with
1
in
T_{0}^{\prime}
, while all other vertex labels remain unchanged.
Hence, in
T
, we have
1\sim v
, and therefore
T\notin\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.
Next, suppose that
r_{j}^{\prime}\neq r^{\prime}
.
If
r_{j}^{\prime}<v
, then the inverse construction again yields
1\sim v
in
T
, and hence
T\notin\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.
Finally, suppose that
r_{j}^{\prime}>v
.
Since
r^{\prime}\sim v
and
r^{\prime}
is not adjacent to any other vertex in
F_{\ell}
, the increasing path from
r^{\prime}
to
r_{j}^{\prime}
in
T^{\prime}
must be of the form

r^{\prime}\rightarrow b_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow b_{t}\rightarrow\rho_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow\rho_{m}\rightarrow r_{j}^{\prime},\quad(t\geq 1),

where
b_{i}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}\setminus\{r^{\prime}\}
and
\rho_{i}\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}
such that

b_{1}<\cdots<b_{t}<\rho_{1}<\cdots<\rho_{m}
.
Since
T^{\prime}
contains no increasing path of the form (
2.1
), it follows that
\rho_{1}>v
.
That is, there exists a path
r^{\prime}\rightarrow b_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow b_{t}\rightarrow\rho
in
T^{\prime}
, where
\rho>v
and the vertex
1
is a descendant of
\rho
.
Under the inverse construction,
r^{\prime}
in
T_{0}^{\prime}
is relabeled as
1
, and since no path of the form (
2.1
) exists and
r_{j}^{\prime}>v
, the vertex
v
is relabeled with the largest vertex in
V(T_{0}^{\prime})\cap\{2,3,\ldots,n-\ell\}
.
Hence, in
T
, the vertex
1
is not adjacent to any vertex in
F_{\ell}
, implying
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.
Therefore, we conclude that if there exists an increasing path

r^{\prime}\rightarrow b_{1}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow b_{t}\rightarrow\rho,\quad(t\geq 1),

in
T^{\prime}
with
b_{i}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}\setminus\{r^{\prime}\}
for
1\leq i\leq t
,
\rho\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}
satisfying
\rho>v
, and vertex
1
is a descendant of
\rho
, then
T^{\prime}\in\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}).

Collecting the above cases, we obtain the stated characterization of the trees in
\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}
.
∎

Remark 2.5
.

From Lemma
2.3
and Proposition
2.4
, we have

\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})=\mathbf{B}\coprod\mathbf{B}^{\prime}\coprod\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}.

Thus, the cardinality of the image set
\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
is obtained by summing the cardinalities of the sets
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
, and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}
.

Proposition 2.6
.

For
n\geq 3
and
1\leq\ell\leq n-2
, we have

(2.2)

\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(n-k-2)(n-2)^{k-1}(n-1)^{n-k-3}=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-2)^{\ell-1}\ell.

Proof.

The identity follows by a straightforward induction on
\ell
.
∎

Note that Proposition
2.6
may be of independent interest and will be used in subsequent sections.

We now compute the cardinality of each of the subsets
\mathbf{B}
,
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
, and
\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}
of
\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
.

Lemma 2.7
.

For
n\geq 3
and
1\leq\ell\leq n-2
, we have

|\mathbf{B}|=(n-\ell-1)\,(n-1)^{\,n-\ell-2}\,(n-2)^{\,\ell-1}\,\ell.

Proof.

By definition,
\mathbf{B}=\{T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}^{1\nsim F_{\ell}}\colon\operatorname{root}(T^{\prime})=r^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}\text{ and }r^{\prime}\nsim j,\forall j>r^{\prime}\}
.
Recall that
\mathbf{B}\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
, and that if
T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}
then its preimage under
\Psi
coincides with
T^{\prime}
.

Fix
T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}
with root
r^{\prime}=n-k
, where
0\leq k\leq\ell-1
.
By definition, the root
r^{\prime}
is not adjacent to any vertex in
\{n-k+1,\,n-k+2,\,\dots,\,n\}
.
We now count the number of such trees
T^{\prime}
for a fixed root
r^{\prime}=n-k
.
Let
\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})
denote the set of all the sons of
r^{\prime}
in
T^{\prime}
, and let
|\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})|=s
.
By definition,
\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-k-1\}
.
Therefore, the set
\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})
can be chosen in
\binom{n-k-2}{s}
ways.
Deleting the vertices
r^{\prime}
and
1
from
T^{\prime}
yields a forest with vertex set
\{2,3,\dots,n\}\setminus\{r^{\prime}\}
and exactly
s
connected components, in which the vertices in
\operatorname{son}_{T^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})
belong to distinct components.
By Theorem
2.2
, the number of such forests
is
s(n-2)^{n-s-3}
.
Finally, the vertex
1
may be attached as a leaf to any vertex in
\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
, giving
n-\ell-1
choices.
Hence, for a fixed
k
, the number of uprooted trees
T^{\prime}
with vertex set
[n]
and root
r^{\prime}=n-k
,
in which the leaf
1
is not adjacent to any vertex in
F_{\ell}
, is given by

\sum_{s=1}^{n-k-2}\binom{n-k-2}{s}~s~(n-2)^{n-s-3}~(n-\ell-1).

Since
0\leq k\leq\ell-1
, summing over all such
k
yields

\displaystyle|\mathbf{B}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}\sum_{s=1}^{n-k-2}\binom{n-k-2}{s}~s~(n-2)^{n-s-3}~(n-\ell-1)

\displaystyle=(n-\ell-1)\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(n-k-2)\left(\sum_{s=1}^{n-k-2}\binom{n-k-3}{s-1}~(n-2)^{n-s-3}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748pt(\text{By setting }s-1=j)

\displaystyle=(n-\ell-1)\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-k-3}\binom{n-k-3}{j}~(n-2)^{n-k-3-j}\right).

Therefore,

(2.3)

|\mathbf{B}|=(n-\ell-1)\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)~(n-1)^{n-k-3}\right).

To evaluate the above sum, we apply Proposition
2.6
.
Combining the identity (
2.2
) and (
2.3
), we obtain

|\mathbf{B}|=(n-\ell-1)\,(n-1)^{\,n-\ell-2}\,(n-2)^{\,\ell-1}\,\ell.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.8
.

For
n\geq 3
and
1\leq\ell\leq n-2
, we have

|\mathbf{B}^{\prime}|=(n-\ell-1)^{2}~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{n-\ell-3}.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
be the set of rooted trees with vertex set
\{2,3,\dots,n\}
and root
r^{\prime}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
such that
r^{\prime}\nsim v
for all
v\in F_{\ell}
.
Observe that any tree in
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
can be obtained from a tree in

\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
by adjoining the vertex
1
as a leaf to a vertex

i\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
of the chosen tree.
Since there are exactly
(n-\ell-1)
choices for such a vertex
i
, we obtain

(2.4)

|\mathbf{B}^{\prime}|=(n-\ell-1)\,|\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}|.

Therefore, to determine the cardinality of
\mathbf{B}^{\prime}
, it suffices to count the elements of
\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
.
Let
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
with
\operatorname{root}{(\widetilde{T}^{\prime})}=r^{\prime}
, and
let
\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}^{\prime}}{(r^{\prime})}=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{s}\}
be the set of sons of
r^{\prime}
in
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
.
Then
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}
has the structure shown in Figure
1
, where for each
1\leq i\leq s
,
\mathcal{F}_{a_{i}}
denotes the rooted forest consisting of all descendants of
a_{i}
, with the roots of its component trees adjacent to
a_{i}
.

r^{\prime}
a_{1}
a_{2}
\dots
a_{s}
\mathcal{F}_{a_{1}}
\mathcal{F}_{a_{2}}
\mathcal{F}_{a_{s}}

Figure 1
.
General structure of a tree
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
with

\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{s}\}
.
Each rectangle
\mathcal{F}_{a_{i}}
represents the rooted forest of descendants of
a_{i}
.

By definition,
\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}^{\prime}}{(r^{\prime})}\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}\setminus\{r^{\prime}\}
.
Hence, the set
\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})
can be chosen in
\binom{n-\ell-2}{s}
ways.
Since
r^{\prime}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
, there are
n-\ell-1
choices for the root
r^{\prime}
.
Moreover,
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}-\{r^{\prime}\}
is a rooted forest with vertex set
\{2,3,\dots,n\}\setminus\{r^{\prime}\}
and
s
connected components (see Figure
2
).
By Theorem
2.2
, the number of such forests is
s(n-2)^{n-s-3}
.
Therefore,

|\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}|=\sum_{s=1}^{n-\ell-2}\binom{n-\ell-2}{s}~s~(n-2)^{n-s-3}~(n-\ell-1).

As
\binom{n-\ell-2}{s}~s=(n-\ell-2)~\binom{n-\ell-3}{s-1}
, we have

(2.5)

|\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}|=(n-\ell-1)~(n-\ell-2)\sum_{s=1}^{n-\ell-2}\binom{n-\ell-3}{s-1}~(n-2)^{n-s-3}.

a_{1}
a_{2}
\dots
a_{s}
\mathcal{F}_{a_{1}}
\mathcal{F}_{a_{2}}
\mathcal{F}_{a_{s}}

Figure 2
.
The forest
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}-\{r^{\prime}\}
, where
\widetilde{T}^{\prime}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{B}}^{\prime}
and

\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}^{\prime}}(r^{\prime})=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{s}\}
.

Thus, by combining (
2.4
) and (
2.5
), we have

\displaystyle|\mathbf{B}^{\prime}|

\displaystyle=(n-\ell-1)^{2}~(n-\ell-2)\left(\sum_{s=1}^{n-\ell-2}\binom{n-\ell-3}{s-1}~(n-2)^{n-s-3}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 256.0748pt(\text{By setting }s-1=j)

\displaystyle=(n-\ell-1)^{2}~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)^{\ell-1}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-\ell-3}\binom{n-\ell-3}{j}~(n-2)^{n-\ell-3-j}\right)

\displaystyle=(n-\ell-1)^{2}~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{n-\ell-3}.

This concludes the proof.
∎

Lemma 2.9
.

For
n\geq 3
and
1\leq\ell\leq n-2
, we have

|\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}|=(n-\ell-1)~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)^{\ell-1}~(n-1)^{n-\ell-3}~\ell.

Proof.

As a first step in computing the cardinality of
\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}
, for each
v\in F_{\ell}
we define a subset
\mathcal{T}_{v}
of
\mathcal{B}_{n}
.
The set
\mathcal{T}_{v}
consists of all trees in
\mathcal{B}_{n}
whose root lies in
\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
and is adjacent to
v
, where
v
is not adjacent to any other vertex in
F_{\ell}
, and the vertex
1
is a descendant of
v
but not adjacent to it.
That is, for
v\in F_{\ell}
, let

\mathcal{T}_{v}=\left\{\,T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{B}_{n}\;\colon\;\begin{aligned} &\operatorname{root}(T^{\prime\prime})=r^{\prime\prime}\in\{2,3,\ldots,n-\ell\},\ r^{\prime\prime}\sim v,\\
&v\nsim w\text{ for all }w\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{v\},1\text{ is a descendant of }v,\text{ and }1\nsim v\end{aligned}\,\right\}.

We recall that there exists a bijection
\Psi:\mathcal{U}_{n}\to\mathcal{B}_{n}
.
We now define a map

(2.6)

\tau:\coprod_{v\in F_{\ell}}\mathcal{T}_{v}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime},

and prove that
\tau
is a bijection.
Since
\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}\subseteq\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})
, we show that every
T^{\prime\prime}\in\coprod_{v\in F_{\ell}}\mathcal{T}_{v}
is mapped by
\tau
to a unique tree

T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}
, where
T^{\prime}=\Psi(T)
for some
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.

The construction of the map (
2.6
) is carried out sequentially.
Let
T^{\prime\prime}\in\coprod_{v\in F_{\ell}}\mathcal{T}_{v}
.
If
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}
, then
\tau
fixes
T^{\prime\prime}
, i.e.,
\tau(T^{\prime\prime})=T^{\prime\prime}
.
Equivalently, if
T^{\prime\prime}=\Psi(T)
for some
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
, then we set
\tau(T^{\prime\prime})=T^{\prime\prime}
.
Now suppose that
T^{\prime\prime}\neq\Psi(T)
for any
T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.
Let
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{v}
with
\operatorname{root}(T^{\prime\prime})=r^{\prime\prime}
.
We proceed through the following cases in order.

(1)

If
1\nsim w
for all
w\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}

We now show that the above procedure is reversible.
Let
T^{\prime}\in\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}
with root
r^{\prime}
.
We claim that there exists a tree

T^{\prime\prime}\in\coprod_{v\in F_{\ell}}\mathcal{T}_{v}
such that
\tau(T^{\prime\prime})=T^{\prime}
.

Suppose that
r^{\prime}
is adjacent to a vertex
v\in F_{\ell}
.
Then, by Proposition
2.4
, the root
r^{\prime}
is not adjacent to any other vertex of
F_{\ell}
.
Starting from
T^{\prime}
, we reconstruct a tree
T^{\prime\prime}
with the required properties by considering the following cases in order.

(i)

We first check whether
T^{\prime}
contains an increasing path of the form

(2.7)

r^{\prime}\longrightarrow d_{1}\longrightarrow d_{2}\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow d_{t}\longrightarrow w,

where
d_{i}\in\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}\setminus\{r^{\prime}\}
for
1\leq i\leq t
, and

w\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{v\}
with
v<w
, such that the vertex
1
is a descendant of
w
.
If no such path exists in
T^{\prime}
, we proceed directly to case
(ii)
.
Otherwise, suppose that a path of the form (
2.7
) exists.
Then, we construct a tree
T^{\prime}_{1}
from
T^{\prime}
as follows.
Remove the vertices
v
and
w
together with their descendant forests
\mathcal{F}_{v}
and
\mathcal{F}_{w}
, respectively.
Then reattach
v
(together with
\mathcal{F}_{v}
) as a son of the vertex
d_{t}
, and
w
(together with
\mathcal{F}_{w}
) as a son of the root
r^{\prime}
, obtaining a tree
T^{\prime}_{1}
.
If
T^{\prime}_{1}\in\mathcal{T}_{w}
, then we set
T^{\prime\prime}=T^{\prime}_{1}
.
Otherwise, if
T^{\prime}_{1}\notin\coprod_{v\in F_{\ell}}\mathcal{T}_{v}
, replace
T^{\prime}
by
T^{\prime}_{1}
(keeping the same root
r^{\prime}
) and proceed to case
(ii)
.

(ii)

We continue with the tree
T^{\prime}
obtained after case
(i)
.
In this tree, the root
r^{\prime}
is adjacent to a vertex
v_{0}\in F_{\ell}
, where
v_{0}=v
or
v_{0}=w
, according to whether an increasing path of the form (
2.7
) existed in the tree considered in case
(i)
.
Moreover, the vertex
1
is a descendant of
v_{0}
.
Consider the subtree of
T^{\prime}
obtained by deleting the son of
v_{0}
lying on the unique path from
v_{0}
to the vertex
1
, together with all of its descendants.
In the resulting subtree, if the vertex
v_{0}
is not adjacent to any vertex in
F_{\ell}
, then proceed directly to case
(iii)
.
Otherwise, suppose that
v_{0}
is adjacent to a non-empty set of vertices
\{w_{1},w_{2},\dots,w_{m}\}\subseteq F_{\ell}\setminus\{v_{0}\}
.
Then, in
T^{\prime}
, for each
1\leq i\leq m
, remove the vertex
w_{i}
together with its descendant forest
\mathcal{F}_{w_{i}}
, and reattach
w_{i}
(together with
\mathcal{F}_{w_{i}}
) as a son of the root
r^{\prime}
.
Let us denote the resulting tree by
T^{\prime}_{2}
.
If
T^{\prime}_{2}\in\coprod_{v\in F_{\ell}}\mathcal{T}_{v}
, then we set
T^{\prime\prime}=T^{\prime}_{2}
.
Otherwise, replace
T^{\prime}
by
T^{\prime}_{2}
(keeping the same root
r^{\prime}
) and proceed to case
(iii)
.

(iii)

Let
\rho
be the son of
v_{0}
lying on the unique path from
v_{0}
to the vertex
1
.
If
\rho\notin F_{\ell}
, then the tree
T^{\prime}
already belongs to
\coprod_{v\in F_{\ell}}\mathcal{T}_{v},
and we set
T^{\prime\prime}=T^{\prime}
.
Otherwise, suppose that
\rho\in F_{\ell}
.
Let
\widetilde{a}
be the parent of the vertex
1
in
T^{\prime}
.
Clearly,
\widetilde{a}\notin F_{\ell}
.
We obtain a tree
T_{3}^{\prime}
from
T^{\prime}
by interchanging the labels of the vertices
\widetilde{a}
and
\rho
.
The tree
T^{\prime}_{3}
thus obtained belongs to
\coprod_{v\in F_{\ell}}\mathcal{T}_{v}
, and we set
T^{\prime\prime}=T_{3}^{\prime}
.

Since all the steps in the construction of the map
\tau\colon\coprod_{v\in F_{\ell}}\mathcal{T}_{v}\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}
can be reversed systematically, the map
\tau
is a bijection.
Note that, for any
v,v^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}
, the set
\mathcal{T}_{v^{\prime}}
can be obtained from
\mathcal{T}_{v}
by interchanging the labels of the vertices
v
and
v^{\prime}
in each tree of
\mathcal{T}_{v}
.
Hence, the cardinality of
\mathcal{T}_{v}
is independent of the choice of
v\in F_{\ell}
.
In particular,
|\mathcal{T}_{v}|=|\mathcal{T}_{n}|
for all
v\in F_{\ell}
.
Therefore,

(2.8)

|\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}|=\bigg|\coprod_{v\in F_{\ell}}\mathcal{T}_{v}\bigg|=\sum_{v\in F_{\ell}}|\mathcal{T}_{v}|=|F_{\ell}|~|\mathcal{T}_{n}|=\ell~|\mathcal{T}_{n}|.

We now proceed to compute the cardinality of the set
\mathcal{T}_{v}
.
Let
\overline{T}
be a tree with vertex set
\{2,3,\dots,n\}
having
\operatorname{root}(\overline{T})=v
, where
v\in F_{\ell}
and
v
is not adjacent to any vertex in
F_{\ell}
.
Let
\operatorname{son}_{\overline{T}}{(v)}=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{s}\}
be the set of sons of
v
in
\overline{T}
, with
|\operatorname{son}_{\overline{T}}{(v)}|=s\geq 2
and
\operatorname{son}_{\overline{T}}{(v)}\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
.
From
\overline{T}
, we construct a tree
\widetilde{T}
with vertex set
[n]
having
\operatorname{root}(\widetilde{T})=v
by attaching the vertex
1
as a leaf, making it a descendant of one of the vertices
a_{i}
for some
1\leq i\leq s
.
Note that,
\operatorname{son}_{\overline{T}}{(v)}=\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}{(v)}
.
If the leaf
1
is a descendant of
a_{j}
in
\widetilde{T}
, then we obtain tree a
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{v}
from
\widetilde{T}
by choosing any vertex in
\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}{(v)}\setminus\{a_{j}\}
as the root (see Figure
6
).
Thus, each tree
\widetilde{T}
gives rise to exactly
s-1
distinct trees in
\mathcal{T}_{v}
.
Conversely, every tree
T^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{T}_{v}
yields a tree
\widetilde{T}
by designating the vertex
v
of
T^{\prime\prime}
as the root.
If the vertex
v
has
s
sons in
T^{\prime\prime}
, then precisely
s-1
trees in
\mathcal{T}_{v}
correspond to the same tree
\widetilde{T}
.

v
a_{1}
\mathcal{F}_{a_{1}}
a_{2}
\mathcal{F}_{a_{2}}
\cdots
a_{j}

\mathcal{F}_{a^{\prime}}
\cdots
a_{s}
\mathcal{F}_{a_{s}}

with
\operatorname{root}(\widetilde{T})=v
and

, where
the leaf
1
is a descendant of
a_{j}
.

, where

.

.
Construction of

from the tree
\widetilde{T}
, where
\operatorname{root}{(T^{\prime\prime})}\in\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}{(v)}\setminus\{a_{j}\}
.

Therefore, by Theorem
2.2
, the number of trees
\overline{T}
with
\operatorname{son}_{\overline{T}}{(v)}=\{a_{1},a_{2},\dots,a_{s}\}
, as described above, is
s(n-2)^{n-3-s}
.
The number of ways to choose a subset
\operatorname{son}_{\overline{T}}{(v)}\subseteq\{2,3,\dots,n-\ell\}
with
s
elements is
\binom{n-\ell-1}{s}
.
For a fixed tree
\overline{T}
, we construct a tree
\widetilde{T}
by attaching the vertex
1
as a leaf.
Since
1
may be attached to any vertex in
\{2,3,\dots,n\}\setminus\{v\}
, there are exactly
n-2
possible choices for this attachment.
Further, as each
\widetilde{T}
yields
s-1
distinct trees in

\mathcal{T}_{v}
, we obtain

|\mathcal{T}_{v}|=\sum_{s=2}^{n-\ell-1}\binom{n-\ell-1}{s}~s~(n-2)^{n-s-3}~(s-1)~(n-2).

Since
\binom{n-\ell-1}{s}~s~(s-1)=(n-\ell-1)~(n-\ell-2)~\binom{n-\ell-3}{s-2}
, we have

\begin{split}|\mathcal{T}_{v}|&=(n-\ell-1)~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)\left(\sum_{s=2}^{n-\ell-1}\binom{n-\ell-3}{s-2}~(n-2)^{n-s-3}\right)\\
&\hskip 256.0748pt(\text{By setting }s-2=j)\\
&=(n-\ell-1)~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-\ell-3}\binom{n-\ell-3}{j}~(n-2)^{n-\ell-3-j}~(n-2)^{\ell-2}\right)\\
&=(n-\ell-1)~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)^{\ell-1}~(n-1)^{n-\ell-3}.\end{split}

Thus, for all
v\in F_{\ell}
, we have

(2.9)

|\mathcal{T}_{v}|=(n-\ell-1)~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)^{\ell-1}~(n-1)^{n-\ell-3}.

Combining (
2.9
) with (
2.8
), we obtain

|\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}|=\ell~|\mathcal{T}_{v}|=(n-\ell-1)~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)^{\ell-1}~(n-1)^{n-\ell-3}~\ell.

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

We are now ready to prove Theorem
2.1
.

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

By Remark
2.5
, we have

|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|=|\Psi(\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}})|=|\mathbf{B}|~+~|\mathbf{B}^{\prime}|~+~|\mathbf{B}^{\prime\prime}|.

Hence, using Lemma
2.7
,
2.8
and
2.9
, we obtain

\begin{split}|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|&=(n-\ell-1)~(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}~(n-2)^{\ell-1}~\ell\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+(n-\ell-1)^{2}~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{n-\ell-3}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+(n-\ell-1)~(n-\ell-2)~(n-2)^{\ell-1}~(n-1)^{n-\ell-3}~\ell\\
&=(n-\ell-1)~(n-1)^{n-\ell-3}~(n-2)^{\ell-1}\Bigl((n-1)~\ell+(n-\ell-2)~(n-1)\Bigr)\\
&=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}~(n-2)^{\ell}~(n-\ell-1).\end{split}

This completes the proof.
∎

3.
Enumeration of
\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
via the matrix tree theorem

For each
0\leq k\leq n-2
, let
G_{\ell}^{n-k}
be the rooted subgraph of
G_{\ell}^{\prime}
with root
n-k
, obtained from
G_{\ell}^{\prime}
by deleting the edges
e_{n-k,i}\in E(G_{\ell}^{\prime})
for all
n-k+1\leq i\leq n
.
That is, for
0\leq k\leq n-2
, we define

G_{\ell}^{n-k}\coloneqq G_{\ell}^{\prime}-\{e_{n-k,i}\ \colon n-k+1\leq i\leq n\}.

Clearly, any spanning tree of
G_{\ell}^{n-k}
is an uprooted spanning tree of
G_{\ell}^{\prime}
with root
n-k
.
Further, recall that
\mathcal{U}(G_{\ell}^{\prime})=\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}
.
Therefore, we can express

\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}=\coprod_{k=0}^{n-2}\mathrm{SPT}{(G_{\ell}^{n-k})}
,
where
\mathrm{SPT}{(G_{\ell}^{n-k})}
denotes the set of all spanning trees of
G_{\ell}^{n-k}
.
Hence, we have

\left|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}\right|=\sum_{k=0}^{n-2}\left|\mathrm{SPT}{(G_{\ell}^{n-k})}\right|.

Further, we can split this sum as follows.

(3.1)

\left|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}\right|=\left|\mathrm{SPT}{(G_{\ell}^{n})}\right|+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell-1}\left|\mathrm{SPT}{(G_{\ell}^{n-k})}\right|+\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-2}\left|\mathrm{SPT}{(G_{\ell}^{n-k})}\right|.

By the matrix tree theorem
[
12
]
, we know that the cardinality of the set of all spanning trees of
G_{\ell}^{n-k}
is given by the determinant of the reduced Laplacian matrix
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
of
G_{\ell}^{n-k}
, i.e.,

(3.2)

|\mathrm{SPT}{(G_{\ell}^{n-k})}|=\det\bigl(\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})\bigr),\quad 0\leq k\leq n-2.

For the graph
G_{\ell}^{n}
with root
n
, we obtain the reduced Laplacian matrix
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n})
by deleting the
n
-th row and the
n
-th column of the Laplacian matrix
L(G_{\ell}^{n})
.
The resulting matrix
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n})
is an
(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrix in which the first diagonal entry is
n-\ell-1
, the
i
-th diagonal entry equals
n-1
for
2\leq i\leq n-\ell
and equals
n-2
for
n-\ell+1\leq i\leq n-1
.
Moreover, all non-diagonal entries are
-1
except for the
(1,j)
-th and
(j,1)
-th entries, where
n-\ell+1\leq j\leq n-1
, which are
0
.
Equivalently,
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n})
is given by

\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n})=\begin{array}[]{*{7}{c}r}&&&\begin{subarray}{c}C_{n-\ell}\\
\downarrow\end{subarray}&&&&\\
n-\ell-1&-1&\cdots&-1&0&\cdots&0&\\
-1&n-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\\
-1&-1&\cdots&n-1&-1&\cdots&-1&\leftarrow\begin{subarray}{c}R_{n-\ell}\\
\end{subarray}\\
0&-1&\cdots&-1&n-2&\cdots&-1&\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\\
0&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\cdots&n-2&{\scriptstyle(n-1)\times(n-1)}&\\
\end{array}.

Let
R_{i}
denote the
i
-th row and
C_{j}
denote the
j
-th column of the matrix
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n})
.
Let us perform the following series of row and column operations on
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n})
.

(1)

C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n-2}C_{i}
,

(2)

C_{i}\longrightarrow C_{i}+C_{1}
, for all
2\leq i\leq n-1
,

(3)

R_{1}\longrightarrow R_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n-\ell}\frac{1}{n}R_{i}
.

After applying these operations in the given order, the matrix
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n})
is reduced to a lower triangular matrix whose first diagonal entry is
\frac{n-\ell-1}{n}
, the
i
-th diagonal entry is
n
for
2\leq i\leq n-\ell
and
n-1
for
n-\ell+1\leq i\leq n-1
.
Since the determinant of a lower triangular matrix is the product of its diagonal entries, and all the above operations preserve the determinant, we have

\det\bigl(\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n})\bigr)=n^{n-\ell-1}~(n-1)^{\ell-1}~\left(\frac{n-\ell-1}{n}\right)=n^{n-\ell-2}~(n-1)^{\ell-1}~(n-\ell-1).

By the matrix tree theorem, we have

(3.3)

|\mathrm{SPT}{(G_{\ell}^{n})}|=\det\bigl(\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n})\bigr)=n^{n-\ell-2}~(n-1)^{\ell-1}~(n-\ell-1).

Now consider the graph
G_{\ell}^{n-k}
with root
n-k
, where
1\leq k\leq\ell-1
.
By construction, in
G_{\ell}^{n-k}
, the vertex
1
is not adjacent to any vertex in
F_{\ell}
, and the root
n-k
is not adjacent to any vertex
i
with
n-k+1\leq i\leq n
.
The reduced Laplacian matrix of
G_{\ell}^{n-k}
is obtained by deleting the
(n-k)
-th row and column of the Laplacian matrix
L(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
.
The resulting reduced Laplacian matrix
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
is an
(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrix in which the first diagonal entry is
n-\ell-1
,
the
i
-th diagonal entry equals
n-1
for
2\leq i\leq n-\ell
, equals
n-2
for
n-\ell+1\leq i\leq n-k-1
, and equals
n-3
for
n-k\leq i\leq n-1
.
Moreover, all non-diagonal entries are
-1
except for the
(1,j)
-th and
(j,1)
-th entries, where
n-\ell+1\leq j\leq n-1
, which are
0
.

Let
R_{i}
and
C_{j}
denote the
i
-th row and
j
-th column of the matrix
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
, respectively.
We now perform the following sequence of row and column operations on
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
.

(1)

C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}C_{i}
,

(2)

C_{i}\longrightarrow C_{i}+C_{1}
, for all
2\leq i\leq n-1
,

(3)

R_{i}\longrightarrow R_{i}-R_{1}
, for all
n-k\leq i\leq n-1
,

(4)

R_{1}\longrightarrow R_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n-\ell}\frac{1}{n}R_{i}
,

(5)

C_{n-k}\longrightarrow C_{n-k}+\sum_{i=n-k+1}^{n-1}C_{i}
,

(6)

C_{i}\longrightarrow C_{i}+\frac{1}{n-k-2}C_{n-k}
, for all
n-k+1\leq i\leq n-1
.

After applying the above operations in the given order, the matrix
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
reduces to a lower triangular matrix whose first diagonal entry is
\frac{n-\ell-1}{n}
,
the
(n-k)
-th diagonal entry is
n-k-2
,
the
i
-th diagonal entry equals
n
for
2\leq i\leq n-\ell
,
equals
n-1
for
n-\ell+1\leq i\leq n-k-1
,
and equals
n-2
for
n-k+1\leq i\leq n-1
.
Since the determinant of a lower triangular matrix is the product of its diagonal entries, and all the above operations preserve the determinant, we obtain

\det\bigl(\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})\bigr)=n^{n-\ell-2}~(n-1)^{\ell-k-1}~(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)~(n-\ell-1).

By the matrix tree theorem, for
1\leq k\leq\ell-1
, we have

(3.4)

|\mathrm{SPT}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})|=n^{n-\ell-2}~(n-1)^{\ell-k-1}~(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)~(n-\ell-1).

For
\ell\leq k\leq n-2
, consider the graph
G_{\ell}^{n-k}
with root
n-k
.
Let
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
be the reduced Laplacian matrix of
G_{\ell}^{n-k}
obtained by deleting the
(n-k)
-th row and column of the Laplacian matrix
L(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
.
Then
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
is an
(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrix whose diagonal entries are given as follows:

d_{ii}=\begin{cases}n-\ell-1,&i=1,\\
n-1,&2\leq i\leq n-k-1,\\
n-2,&n-k\leq i\leq n-\ell-1,\\
n-3,&n-\ell\leq i\leq n-1.\end{cases}

All non-diagonal entries are equal to
-1
, except the
(1,j)
-th and
(j,1)
-th entries, where
n-\ell\leq j\leq n-1
, which are equal to
0
.

Let
R_{i}
and
C_{j}
denote the
i
-th row and
j
-th column of
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
, respectively.
We now perform the following column operations on
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
.

(1)

C_{1}\longrightarrow C_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n-1}C_{i}
,

(2)

C_{i}\longrightarrow C_{i}+C_{1}
, for all
2\leq i\leq n-\ell-1
.

Applying these operations in the stated order transforms the matrix
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})
into the
(n-1)\times(n-1)
matrix
\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})^{\prime}
given by

\scalebox{0.84}{$\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})^{\prime}=\begin{array}[]{cccccccccccccccc}&&&&&\begin{subarray}{c}C_{n-k-1}\\
\downarrow\end{subarray}&&&&\begin{subarray}{c}C_{n-\ell-1}\\
\downarrow\end{subarray}&&&&&\begin{subarray}{c}C_{n-1}\\
\downarrow\end{subarray}\\
&1&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0&0&\\
&1&n&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\\
&1&0&n&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\\
&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\\
&1&0&0&\cdots&n&0&0&\cdots&0&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\longleftarrow\begin{subarray}{c}R_{n-k-1}\end{subarray}\\
&0&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&n-2&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\\
&0&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&n-2&\cdots&-1&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\\
&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\\
&0&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&-1&\cdots&n-2&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\longleftarrow\begin{subarray}{c}R_{n-\ell-1}\end{subarray}\\
&0&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&n-3&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\\
&0&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&n-3&\cdots&-1&-1&\\
&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\\
&0&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\cdots&n-3&-1&\\
&0&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&-1&\cdots&-1&n-3&\longleftarrow\begin{subarray}{c}R_{n-1}\end{subarray}\\
\end{array}$}$$Sincealltheabovecolumnoperationspreservethedeterminant,wehave$\det\bigl(\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})\bigr)=\det\bigl(\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})'\bigr)$.Wecomputethedeterminantofthematrix$\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})'$byexpandingalongthefirstrow,whoseonlynonzeroentryisthefirstentry,equalto$1$.Hence,$$\det\bigl(\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})^{\prime}\bigr)=\det\bigl(\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})^{\prime\prime}\bigr),$$where$\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})''$isthe$(n-2)\times(n-2)$matrixobtainedfrom$\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})'$bydeletingitsfirstrowandfirstcolumn.Applyingtherowoperations$R_i \longrightarrow R_i - R_{n-2}$,whichpreservethedeterminant,forall$n-k-1\leq i\leq n-3$,transformsthematrix$\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})''$intotheblockmatrix$$\widetilde{\mathbf{L}}=\begin{bNiceArray}_{(n-2)\times(n-2)},$$where$A$isan$(n-\ell-2)\times(n-\ell-2)$diagonalmatrixwhose$i$-thdiagonalentryequals$n$for$1\leq i \leq n-k-2$,andequals$n-1$for$n-k-1\leq i\leq n-\ell-2$.Further,$B=[b_{ij}]$isan$(n-\ell-2)\times\ell$matrixwithentries$$b_{ij}=\begin{cases}-1,&1\leq i\leq n-k-2,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell,\\
0,&n-k-1\leq i\leq n-\ell-2,\ 1\leq j\leq\ell-1,\\
-(n-2),&n-k-1\leq i\leq n-\ell-2,\ j=\ell.\end{cases}$$Moreover,$C$isan$\ell\times(n-\ell-2)$matrixwhoseentriesareall$0$,exceptthoseinthelastrow,whichareallequalto$-1$.Finally,$D$isan$\ell\times\ell$matrixwhosediagonalentriesareallequalto$n-2$,exceptthelastdiagonalentry,whichisequalto$n-3$,andwhosenon-diagonalentriesareall$0$,exceptthe$(i,\ell)$-thentries,whichareequalto$-(n-2)$for$1 \leq i \leq\ell-1$,andthe$(\ell,j)$-thentries,whichareequalto$-1$for$1 \leq j \leq\ell-1$.\par As$A$isinvertible,usingtheSchur^{\prime}sformulafordeterminantsofblockmatrices,wehave$$\det\bigl(\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})\bigr)=\det\bigl(\widetilde{\mathbf{L}}\bigr)=\det(A)\cdot\det(D-CA^{-1}B).$$As$A$isdiagonal,weobtain$\det(A) = n^{n-k-2}\, (n-1)^{k-\ell} $,andthematrix$D-CA^{-1}B$isgivenby\begin{equation*}D-CA^{-1}B=\begin{array}[]{*{6}{c}r}n-2&0&0&\cdots&0&-(n-2)&\\
0&n-2&0&\ddots&0&-(n-2)&\\
0&0&n-2&\cdots&0&-(n-2)&\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\\
0&0&0&\cdots&n-2&-(n-2)&\\
\frac{-2n+k+2}{n}&\frac{-2n+k+2}{n}&\frac{-2n+k+2}{n}&\cdots&\frac{-2n+k+2}{n}&n-3-\lambda&{\scriptstyle\ell\times\ell}\\
\end{array},\end{equation*}where$\lambda= \frac{n-k-2}{n} + \frac{(k-\ell)(n-2)}{n-1}$.Nowapplythecolumnoperation$C_{\ell} \longrightarrow C_{\ell} +\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1} C_{i}$.Thenthematrix$D-CA^{-1}B$istransformedintoalowertriangularmatrixwhosediagonalentriesareallequalto$n-2$,exceptthelastdiagonalentry,whichisequalto$(n-k-\ell-2)+\frac{k-\ell}{n-1}+\frac{\ell(k+2)}{n}$.Sincetheabovecolumnoperationpreservesthedeterminant,andthedeterminantofatriangularmatrixistheproductofitsdiagonalentries,itfollowsthat$$\det(D-CA^{-1}B)=(n-2)^{\ell-1}~\bigg((n-k-\ell-2)+\frac{k-\ell}{n-1}+\frac{\ell(k+2)}{n}\bigg).$$Hence,weobtain$$\det\bigl(\widetilde{L}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})\bigr)=n^{n-k-2}~(n-1)^{k-\ell}~(n-2)^{\ell-1}~\bigg((n-k-\ell-2)+\frac{k-\ell}{n-1}+\frac{\ell(k+2)}{n}\bigg).$$For$\ell\leq k\leq n-2$,againbythematrixtreetheorem,wehave\begin{equation}|\mathrm{SPT}(G_{\ell}^{n-k})|=n^{n-k-2}~(n-1)^{k-\ell}~(n-2)^{\ell-1}~\bigg((n-k-\ell-2)+\frac{k-\ell}{n-1}+\frac{\ell~(k+2)}{n}\bigg).\end{equation}\par Now,from\eqref{eq:Uprooted sum MTT},\eqref{eq:MTT_root_n},\eqref{eq:MTT_root_n-k (k<l)}and\eqref{eq:MTT_root_n-k (k>l)},weobtain\begin{equation*}\begin{split}|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|&=n^{n-\ell-2}~(n-1)^{\ell-1}~(n-\ell-1)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{k=1}^{\ell-1}n^{n-\ell-2}~(n-1)^{\ell-k-1}~(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)~(n-\ell-1)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-2}n^{n-k-2}~(n-1)^{k-\ell}~(n-2)^{\ell-1}\left((n-k-\ell-2)+\frac{k-\ell}{n-1}+\frac{\ell~(k+2)}{n}\right).\end{split}\end{equation*}Thisexpressioncanberewrittenas\begin{equation}\begin{split}|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|&=n^{n-\ell-2}~(n-\ell-1)\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{\ell-k-1}~(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)\\
&\hskip 5.69046pt+(n-2)^{\ell-1}\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-2}n^{n-k-2}~(n-1)^{k-\ell}\left((n-k-\ell-2)+\frac{(\ell+1)~nk+\ell~(n-k-2)}{n~(n-1)}\right).\end{split}\end{equation}\par\par Wenowpresentafewpropositionsthatwillenableustocombinetheprecedingresultsanddeterminethecardinalityoftheset$\mathcal{U}_n^{1\not\sim F_\ell}$asanapplicationofthematrixtreetheorem.\par\begin{proposition}Let $\ell\geq 1$. Then, for all $n\geq\ell+2$, we have
\begin{equation*}\begin{split}\ell\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-\ell-2}+\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-2}\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-k-2}\left(\frac{n-k-\ell-2}{n-\ell-1}+\frac{(\ell+1)~nk~+\ell~(n-k-2)}{n~(n-1)~(n-\ell-1)}\right)=n-2.\end{split}\end{equation*}\end{proposition}\par\par\@proof Letusintroduceanauxiliaryrealparameter$a\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\lx@text@lbrace 0,1,\ell+1\lx@text@rbrace$andconsiderthemoregeneralidentity\begin{equation}\begin{split}&\ell\left(\frac{a}{a-1}\right)^{n-\ell-2}+\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-2}\left(\frac{a}{a-1}\right)^{n-k-2}\left(\frac{a-k-\ell-2}{a-\ell-1}+\frac{(\ell+1)~ak~+\ell~(a-k-2)}{a~(a-1)~(a-\ell-1)}\right)\\
&\hskip 227.62204pt=(n-2)+\left(1+\frac{\ell n-(n-1)~a}{a~(a-\ell-1)}\right).\end{split}\end{equation}Identity\eqref{eq:generalidentity}canbeverifiedbyinductionon$n$.Bysubstituting$a=n$into\eqref{eq:generalidentity},weobtaintherequiredresultforallintegers$n\geq\ell+2$.\qed\end@proof\par\par\begin{proposition}Let $\ell\geq 1$. Then, for all $n\geq\ell+2$, we have
\begin{equation}\begin{split}(n-2)^{\ell}&=\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-\ell-2}~\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{\ell-k-1}~(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\frac{(n-2)^{\ell-1}}{n-\ell-1}~\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-2}\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-k-2}\left((n-k-\ell-2)+\frac{(\ell+1)~nk+\ell~(n-k-2)}{n~(n-1)}\right).\end{split}\end{equation}\end{proposition}\par\@proof Dividingbothsidesof\eqref{eq:|B|.2}by$(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}$,weobtain$$\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{\ell-k-1}~(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)=\ell~(n-2)^{\ell-1}.$$Hence,$$\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-\ell-2}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{\ell-k-1}~(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)\right)=\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-\ell-2}\ell~(n-2)^{\ell-1}.$$Therefore,\begin{aligned} &\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-\ell-2}\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{\ell-k-1}~(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)\right)$}\\
&\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(n-2)^{\ell-1}}{n-\ell-1}\left(\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-2}\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-k-2}\left((n-k-\ell-2)+\frac{(\ell+1)~nk+\ell~(n-k-2)}{n~(n-1)}\right)\right)$}\\
&\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle=(n-2)^{\ell-1}\Biggl[\ell\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-\ell-2}+\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-2}\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-k-2}\left(\frac{n-k-\ell-2}{n-\ell-1}+\frac{(\ell+1)~nk+\ell~(n-k-2)}{n~(n-1)~(n-\ell-1)}\right)\Biggr]$}.\end{aligned}ByProposition~\ref{prop:(n-2)},thequantityinsidethebracketsisequalto$n-2$.Hence,\begin{aligned} &\scalebox{0.9}{$\displaystyle(n-2)^{\ell-1}\Biggl[\ell\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-\ell-2}+\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-2}\left(\frac{n}{n-1}\right)^{n-k-2}\left(\frac{n-k-\ell-2}{n-\ell-1}+\frac{(\ell+1)~nk+\ell~(n-k-2)}{n~(n-1)~(n-\ell-1)}\right)\Biggr]$}\\
&=(n-2)^{\ell-1}\,(n-2)\\
&=(n-2)^{\ell}.\end{aligned}Thisconcludestheproof.\qed\end@proof\par Multiplyingbothsidesoftheidentity\eqref{eq:(n-2)l_identity}by$(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}~(n-\ell-1)$,weobtaintheequivalentform\begin{equation}\begin{split}&(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}~(n-2)^{\ell}~(n-\ell-1)\\
&=n^{n-\ell-2}~(n-\ell-1)\sum_{k=0}^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{\ell-k-1}~(n-2)^{k-1}~(n-k-2)\\
&\hskip 5.69046pt+(n-2)^{\ell-1}\sum_{k=\ell}^{n-2}n^{n-k-2}~(n-1)^{k-\ell}\left((n-k-\ell-2)+\frac{(\ell+1)~nk+\ell~(n-k-2)}{n~(n-1)}\right),\end{split}\end{equation}validfor$\ell\geq 1$and$n \geq\ell+2$.\par Combining\eqref{eq:Un_MTTsum01}with\eqref{eq:Un_MTTsum},werecovertheformula$$|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}~(n-2)^{\ell}~(n-\ell-1).$$ThisprovidesanalternativeproofofTheorem~\ref{thm:|Un|},asanapplicationofthematrixtreetheorem.\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{\texorpdfstring{Spherical $\Gl$-parking function}{Spherical Gl parking function}}
\par Having enumerated the uprooted spanning trees of $G_{\ell}^{\prime}$ in the preceding sections, we now turn to the study of spherical $G_{\ell}$-parking functions.
In this section, we recall the necessary background, summarize the known results, and establish several results that will be used in Section~\ref{sec:Counting SPF(Gl)}.
\par\par Recall that $K_{n+1}$ denotes the complete graph with vertex set $\{0\}\cup[n]$ and designated root $0$.
The graph $G_{\ell}$ is obtained from $K_{n+1}$ by deleting the $\ell$ edges joining vertex $1$ to the vertices in $F_{\ell}=\{n-\ell+1,\ldots,n\}$, and we set $G_{\ell}^{\prime}=G_{\ell}-\{0\}$.
For a vertex $r\in[n]$, we write $(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$ to denote the graph $G_{\ell}^{\prime}$ with root $r$.
\par We recall the Depth-First-Search burning algorithm of Perkinson, Yang, and Yu~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{PYY17}{}{}]}.
Applied to the rooted graph $(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$ with input function $P\colon[n]\setminus\{r\}\to\mathbb{N}$, the algorithm simulates a fire starting at $r$ and spreading through $G_{\ell}^{\prime}$ according to the depth-first-search rule, where $P(j)$ represents the number of water droplets at vertex $j$ available to resist the fire.
A vertex is said to be \emph{burnt} once it has been reached by the fire.
At each step, if the largest unburnt vertex $j$ adjacent to a burnt vertex $i$ has $P(j)=0$, the fire spreads to $j$, and the edge $e_{i,j}$ is recorded.
Otherwise, one water droplet at $j$ is consumed, the edge $e_{i,j}$ is declared \emph{dampened} and removed, and the fire continues to spread from $i$.
When all edges from a burnt vertex $i$ to unburnt vertices have been dampened, the algorithm \emph{backtracks} to the previous burnt vertex in the depth-first-search order.
If all vertices of $G_{\ell}^{\prime}$ are eventually burnt, then $P$ is a $(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$-parking function and the tree edges form a spanning tree of $(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$, yielding a bijection $\phi\colon\mathrm{PF}(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)\to\mathrm{SPT}(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$.
\par\par Let $\mathcal{M}_{G_{\ell}}=\bigl\langle m_{A}:\emptyset\neq A\subseteq[n]\bigr\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{K}[x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}]$ be the $G_{\ell}$-parking function ideal, and note that $m_{[n]}=x_{1}x_{2}\cdots x_{n}$.
For $\mathcal{P}\colon[n]\to\mathbb{N}$, we write
$\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}=\prod_{i\in[n]}x_{i}^{\mathcal{P}(i)}$ for the associated monomial.
Following \cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CGS}{}{}]}, for $\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})$, we define the \emph{reduced spherical $G_{\ell}$-parking function} $\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\colon[n]\to\mathbb{N}$ by $\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(i)=\mathcal{P}(i)-1$ for all $i\in[n]$, and denote the set of all such functions by $\widetilde{\mathrm{sPF}}(G_{\ell})$.
\par\par\begin{theorem}Let $G_{\ell}$ be as above, with root $0$ connected to all other vertices.
Then,
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@[\rm{(1)}]$\widetilde{\mathrm{sPF}}(G_{\ell})\subseteq\mathrm{PF}(G_{\ell})$.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[\rm{(2)}]Let $\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})$ and let $r\in[n]$ be the unique vertex such that $\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(r)=0$ but $\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(j)\geq 1$ for all $j>r$.
Consider the graph $(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$ with vertex set $[n]$ and root $r$.
Then
$\widehat{\mathcal{P}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}|_{[n]\setminus\{r\}}$
is a $(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$-parking function.
\end{enumerate}\end{theorem}
\par This enables us to use a modified Depth-First-Search burning algorithm~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CGS}{}{}]} to associate uprooted spanning trees of $G_{\ell}^{\prime}$ with spherical $G_{\ell}$-parking functions.
We briefly recall this algorithm following~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CGS}{}{}]}.
Let $\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})$ and let $\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}$ be the associated reduced spherical $G_{\ell}$-parking function.
Set $r=\max\{i\in[n]:\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}(i)=0\}$ and consider the graph $G_{\ell}^{\prime}$ with root $r$.
Since $\widehat{\mathcal{P}}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}|_{[n]\setminus\{r\}}$ is a $(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$-parking function by Theorem~\ref{thm:reducedSPF}, the Depth-First-Search burning algorithm yields a spanning tree $\phi(\widehat{\mathcal{P}})\in\mathrm{SPT}(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$.
Moreover, since $\widehat{\mathcal{P}}(j)\geq 1$ for all $j>r$, all edges $e_{r,j}$ for $j>r$ get dampened during the algorithm, and hence
$\phi(\widehat{\mathcal{P}})$ is an uprooted spanning tree of $G_{\ell}^{\prime}$.
Recall that $\mathcal{U}(G_{\ell}^{\prime})=\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}$.
The modified Depth-First-Search burning algorithm induces a map
$$\phi_{G_{\ell}}\colon\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}},\qquad\phi_{G_{\ell}}(\mathcal{P})=\phi(\widehat{\mathcal{P}}).$$
In particular, we have the following result.
\par\par\begin{theorem}There exists an injective map
$$\phi_{G_{\ell}}\colon\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}.$$\end{theorem}
\par\par Now, we proceed to give the description of $\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})$.
Let $T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}$ be an uprooted spanning tree of $G_{\ell}^{\prime}$ with $\operatorname{root}(T)=r$.
Since the Depth-First-Search burning algorithm induces a bijection $\phi\colon\mathrm{PF}(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)\to\mathrm{SPT}(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$, there exists a unique $(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$-parking function $\mathcal{P}_{T}$ such that $\phi(\mathcal{P}_{T})=T$.
By~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CGS}{}{}]}*{Proposition~21}, we have $\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})\subseteq\{T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}:\mathcal{P}_{T}(j)\geq 1\text{ for all }j>r=\operatorname{root}(T)\}.$
We define
$$\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}=\{T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}:\operatorname{root}(T)=r\in F_{\ell}\setminus\{n-\ell+1\}\text{ and }\mathcal{P}_{T}(k)\geq 1\text{ for all }2\leq k\leq n-\ell\},$$
and express $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}$ as a disjoint union $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}=\coprod_{p=0}^{\ell-2}\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$, where $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}=\{T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}:\operatorname{root}(T)=n-p\}$.
Note that for $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$, the condition $\mathcal{P}_{T}(k)\geq 1$ for all $2\leq k\leq n-\ell$ implies that $\mathcal{P}_{T}(j)=0$ for some $j\in\{n-\ell+1,\ldots,n-p-1\}$.
The following theorem gives a description of $\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})$.
\par\par\begin{theorem}For $n\geq 3$ and $1\leq\ell\leq n-2$, we have
$$\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})=\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}.$$\end{theorem}
\par\par\par\@proof We first show that $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}\cap\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})=\emptyset$.
Suppose, to the contrary, that $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}\cap\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})$, and let $\operatorname{root}(T)=r$.
Let $\mathcal{P}_{T}$ be the unique $(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$-parking function such that $\phi(\mathcal{P}_{T})=T$.
Since $T\in\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})$, there exists $\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})$ such that $\phi_{G_{\ell}}(\mathcal{P})=T$.
By definition of $\phi_{G_{\ell}}$, we have $\mathcal{P}_{T}=\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}\big|_{[n]\setminus\{r\}}$,
where $\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}$ is the reduced spherical $G_{\ell}$-parking function associated with $\mathcal{P}$.
Hence, $\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}=m_{[n]}\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}_{T}}$.
Since $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}$, we have $\prod_{i=2}^{n-\ell}x_{i}$ divides $\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}_{T}}$, i.e., $\prod_{i=2}^{n-\ell}x_{i}\mid\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}_{T}}$.
For $A=\{2,3,\ldots,n\}$, we have $m_{[n]}\prod_{i=2}^{n-\ell}x_{i}=x_{1}m_{A}$
and hence $m_{A}\mid\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}$, contradicting that $\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})$.
Therefore, $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}\cap\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})=\emptyset$, and hence $\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})\subseteq\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}$.
\par For the reverse inclusion, we partition $\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}$ into
two disjoint subsets as follows.
\@@amsalign
We show that $\mathcal{A}_{1}\coprod\mathcal{A}_{2}\subseteq\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})$.
Let $T\in\mathcal{A}_{1}\coprod\mathcal{A}_{2}$ with $\operatorname{root}(T)=r$, and let
$\mathcal{P}_{T}$ be the corresponding $(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$-parking function such that $\phi(\mathcal{P}_{T})=T$.
Set $\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}=m_{[n]}\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}_{T}}$.
We claim that $\mathcal{P}\in\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})$, i.e.,
$\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{M}_{G_{\ell}}\setminus\mathcal{M}_{G_{\ell}}^{(n-2)}$.
Since $m_{[n]}\mid\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}$, clearly $\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{M}_{G_{\ell}}$.
Suppose, for contradiction, that $\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}\in\mathcal{M}_{G_{\ell}}^{(n-2)}$.
Then there exists a non-empty proper subset $A$ of $[n]$ such that
$m_{A}\mid\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}$.
If $r\notin A$, then
$m_{A}\mid\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}$ implies
$\frac{m_{A}}{\gcd(m_{A},m_{[n]})}\mid\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}_{T}}$,
contradicting that $\mathcal{P}_{T}\in\mathrm{PF}(G_{\ell}^{\prime};r)$.
Now suppose that $r\in A$.
If $T\in\mathcal{A}_{1}$, then since the degree of $x_{r}$ in $\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}$ is $1$
and $m_{A}\mid\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}$,
we must have $A=[n]$, contradicting that $A$ is a proper subset of $[n]$.
If $T\in\mathcal{A}_{2}$, then we have $r\in A\cap F_{\ell}$ and $1\nsim r$.
Since the degree of $x_{r}$ in $\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}$ is $1$,
we obtain $A=[n]\setminus\{1\}$ and
$m_{A}=\prod_{2\leq i\leq n-\ell}x_{i}^{2}\prod_{j\in F_{\ell}}x_{j}$.
As $T\in\mathcal{A}_{2}$, there exists $k\in\{2,\ldots,n-\ell\}$ such that the degree of $x_{k}$ in $\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}$ is $1$.
Thus, $m_{A}$ does not divide $\mathbf{x}^{\mathcal{P}}$, a contradiction.
Therefore, $\mathcal{A}_{1}\coprod\mathcal{A}_{2}\subseteq\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})$, and this completes the proof.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\par For $\ell=1$, the map $\phi_{G_{\ell}}$ was shown to be surjective in~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CGS}{}{}]}, and hence $\mathcal{C}_{n,1}=\emptyset$.
Therefore, for the rest of this article, we assume $\ell\geq 2$.
We now give a more geometric description of the set $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ by introducing the notion of a maximum path in $F_{\ell}$ associated with an uprooted tree, and fixing notation that will be used throughout.
Recall that $\mathcal{U}_{n}$ denotes the set of all uprooted trees with vertex set $[n]$.
\par\par Let $T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}$ be an uprooted tree with $\operatorname{root}(T)=r\in F_{\ell}$, and let the largest son of $r$ in $T$ be a vertex $a_{1}\in F_{\ell}$ satisfying $a_{1}<r$.
The \emph{maximum path of $T$ in $F_{\ell}$}, denoted by $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)$, is the path
$$r=a_{0}\to a_{1}\to a_{2}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha-1}\to a_{\alpha}\qquad(\alpha\geq 1),$$
satisfying the following conditions:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$a_{i}\in F_{\ell}$ for all $0\leq i\leq\alpha$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@for each $1\leq i\leq\alpha$, the vertex $a_{i}$ is the largest son of $a_{i-1}$ in $T$,
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@$a_{1}<r=a_{0}$, and
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@either $a_{\alpha}$ is a leaf of $T$, or every son of $a_{\alpha}$ belongs to $\{2,\ldots,n-\ell\}$, i.e., $\operatorname{son}_{T}(a_{\alpha})\cap F_{\ell}=\emptyset$.
\end{enumerate}
\par\par For a vertex $v$ of a rooted tree $T$, let $\mathcal{F}_{v}(T)$, or simply $\mathcal{F}_{v}$ when $T$ is clear from the context, denote the rooted forest formed by all descendants of $v$, where the roots of the connected components are precisely the sons of $v$ in $T$.
More generally, for any vertex $v$ of $T$, the notation $\mathcal{F}_{(v)}(T)$ denotes the rooted forest of descendants of $v$ with certain subtrees removed, where the excluded subtrees will be clear from the context.
The rooted subtree of $T$ consisting of $v$ together with all of its
descendants, rooted at $v$, is $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{v}(T)=v\vee\mathcal{F}_{v}(T)$ (see, Figure~\ref{fig:vFv_structure}).

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\par\vskip 8.53581pt\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{(c)}}{When $w$ is a descendant of $v$ but not a leaf.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\small(c)}}{\small When $w$ is a descendant of $v$ but not a leaf.}}}\@add@centering\end{subfigure}\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{7}}{Possible structures of the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{v}(T)$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 7}}{\small Possible structures of the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{v}(T)$.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\par\par For the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T):a_{0}\to a_{1}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha}$, let $\mathcal{F}_{(a_{i})}(T)$ denote the rooted forest obtained from $\mathcal{F}_{a_{i}}(T)$ by removing the subtree rooted at $a_{i+1}$.
Equivalently, the roots of the connected components of $\mathcal{F}_{(a_{i})}(T)$ are precisely the sons of $a_{i}$ other than $a_{i+1}$.
\par\par Let $u$ and $v$ be vertices of the rooted tree $T$ such that $u$ is a leaf of $T$ and is not a descendant of $v$.
We construct a new rooted tree $T^{\prime}$ by removing the forest $\mathcal{F}_{v}(T)$ from $T$ and attaching each root of $\mathcal{F}_{v}(T)$ to the vertex $u$.
We denote this operation by
$$T^{\prime}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{v}(T)\to u\bigr],$$
and say that $T^{\prime}$ is obtained from $T$ by \emph{attaching the descendants of $v$ to $u$}.
We note that this operation is defined only when $u$ is a leaf of $T$ and is not a descendant of $v$.
This operation is illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:T to T'}.
By construction, $\mathcal{F}_{u}(T^{\prime})=\mathcal{F}_{v}(T),$ and hence $T=T^{\prime}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{u}(T^{\prime})\to v\bigr].$
Thus, the transformation $T\mapsto T^{\prime}$ is reversible.
