\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{8}}{The operation $T^{\prime}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{v}(T)\to u\bigr]$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 8}}{\small The operation $T^{\prime}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{v}(T)\to u\bigr]$.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\par\par\begin{proposition}Let $T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}$. Then $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ if and only if the following conditions are satisfied.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\operatorname{root}(T)=r=n-p$ for some integer $p$ with
$0\leq p\leq\ell-2$, and
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)$ of $T$ in $F_{\ell}$ is of the form
\begin{equation}n-p=a_{0}\to a_{1}\to a_{2}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha-1}\to a_{\alpha}\qquad(\alpha\geq 1),\end{equation}where $a_{\alpha}$ is a leaf of $T$.
\end{enumerate}\end{proposition}
\par\par\@proof Let $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
Then $\operatorname{root}(T)=r=n-p$ for some $0\leq p\leq\ell-2$, and $\mathcal{P}_{T}(k)\geq 1$ for all $2\leq k\leq n-\ell.$
Moreover, $\mathcal{P}_{T}(k)\geq 1$ for all $r<k\leq n$.
Therefore, proceeding as per the Depth-First-Search burning algorithm, we see that the largest son $a_{1}$ of $r$ satisfies $n-\ell+1\leq a_{1}<r$.
Suppose the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)$ has the form \eqref{eq:Pmax_path}.
We claim that $a_{\alpha}$ is a leaf of $T$.
Since $\mathcal{P}_{T}(k)\geq 1$ for all $2\leq k\leq n-\ell$, every edge joining $a_{\alpha}$ to a vertex $k\in\{2,\dots,n-\ell\}$ is dampened during the Depth-First-Search burning algorithm.
Thus, the algorithm backtracks from $a_{\alpha}$, and hence $a_{\alpha}$ must be a leaf.
\par Conversely, suppose that $T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}$ satisfies conditions \ref{cond1} and \ref{cond2}.
Then $\operatorname{root}(T)=n-p$ for some $0\leq p\leq\ell-2$.
Since $a_{\alpha}$ is a leaf, the Depth-First-Search burning algorithm backtracks from $a_{\alpha}$, which implies $\mathcal{P}_{T}(k)\geq 1$ for all $2\leq k\leq n-\ell$. Therefore $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par See Figure~\ref{fig:Cnlp_structure} for an illustration of the structure of trees in $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{9}}{A tree $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ with maximum path
$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T):r\to a_{1}\to a_{2}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha}$ and the associated forests.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 9}}{\small A tree $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ with maximum path
$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T):r\to a_{1}\to a_{2}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha}$ and the associated forests.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\par\par\begin{proposition}Let $0\leq p\leq\ell-2$, and let $T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}$ be an uprooted tree with $\operatorname{root}(T)=n-p$.
Suppose that $T$ has the following properties.
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@[\rm(1)]The maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)$ of $T$ in $F_{\ell}$ is of the form $n-p=a_{0}\to a_{1}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha-1}\to a_{\alpha},$
where $\alpha\geq 1$ and $a_{1}<n-p$,
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[\rm(2)]vertex $1$ is a leaf of $T$ and is not adjacent to any vertex in $F_{\ell}$, and
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@[\rm(3)]$1$ is not a descendant of $a_{\alpha}$ in $T$.
\end{enumerate}Let $(T)^{*}$ be the tree obtained from $T$ by attaching the descendants
$\mathcal{F}_{a_{\alpha}}(T)$ of $a_{\alpha}$ to the leaf $1$, that is,
$$(T)^{*}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{a_{\alpha}}(T)\to 1\bigr].$$Then $(T)^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
\end{proposition}
\par\par\@proof In the tree $(T)^{*}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{a_{\alpha}}(T)\to 1\bigr]$,
the vertex $a_{\alpha}$ becomes a leaf.
Moreover, since the roots of the connected components of $\mathcal{F}_{a_{\alpha}}(T)$ do not belong to $F_{\ell}$, the vertex $1$ remains non-adjacent to every vertex in $F_{\ell}$.
Hence, $(T)^{*}\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}$.
Further, the maximum path of $(T)^{*}$ in $F_{\ell}$ remains $n-p=a_{0}\to a_{1}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha},$ with $a_{\alpha}$ now a leaf of $(T)^{*}$.
Therefore, all the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:Cnlp_characterization} are satisfied, and hence $(T)^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\par The results established in this section will be used in Section~\ref{sec:Counting SPF(Gl)} to enumerate $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$, and thereby determine $|\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})|$.
\par\par\par
\par\@@numbered@section{section}{toc}{\texorpdfstring{Enumeration of Spherical $\Gl$-parking function}{Counting Spherical Parking Function}}
\par\par Our goal in this section is to enumerate the spherical $G_{\ell}$-parking functions.
By Theorems~\ref{thm:SPFGl_to_UnFl} and~\ref{thm:Im(Psi)}, this reduces to computing $|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}|$.
Since the value of $|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|$ was determined in the preceding sections, it remains to enumerate $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}$.
Recall that $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}=\coprod_{p=0}^{\ell-2}\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
Thus, it suffices to determine $|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}|$ for each $0\leq p\leq\ell-2$.
As counting $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ directly is difficult, we first establish several intermediate results that will lead to an explicit formula for $|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}|$, and hence for $|\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})|$.
Henceforth, we assume that $n\geq\ell+2$ and $\ell\geq 2$.
\par\par Let $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$ denote the set of trees $\widetilde{T}$ with vertex set $[n]$ satisfying the following conditions:
\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@$\widetilde{T}$ is uprooted with $\operatorname{root}(\widetilde{T})=r=n-\ell+2,$
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@vertex $1$ is not adjacent to the root $r$, and
\par\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@vertex $n-\ell+1$ is a leaf adjacent to the root $r$.
\end{enumerate}
\par For $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$, let $\mathcal{F}_{(r)}(\widetilde{T})$ denote the rooted forest consisting of all descendants of the root $r$ in $\widetilde{T}$, excluding the leaf $n-\ell+1$, and let $S=\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(r)\setminus\{n-\ell+1\}$
be the set of sons of the root $r$ other than the leaf $n-\ell+1$.
Then the roots of the connected components of the forest $\mathcal{F}_{(r)}(\widetilde{T})$ are precisely the vertices in $S$, and $\emptyset\neq S\subseteq\{2,3,\ldots,n-\ell\}.$
Thus, every tree $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$ has the form illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:Tnl_structure}.
\begin{figure}\centering\hbox to430.86pt{\vbox to104.58pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 119.05843pt\lower-90.62738pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
\par{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@fill\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -60.64 0 C -60.64 1.3 -61.7 2.35 -62.99 2.35 C -64.29 2.35 -65.34 1.3 -65.34 0 C -65.34 -1.3 -64.29 -2.35 -62.99 -2.35 C -61.7 -2.35 -60.64 -1.3 -60.64 0 Z M -62.99 0}{stroke:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-45.52458pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{}{{{{{}}{}{}{}{}{{}}}}}{{}}{}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -64.79 -1.91 L -108.44 -48.48}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{10}}{The structure of a tree $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 10}}{\small The structure of a tree $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\par\begin{proposition}The cardinality of $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$ is given by
$$|\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}|=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}~(n-2)^{\ell-2}~(n-\ell-1).$$\end{proposition}
\par\par\@proof Every tree $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$ is uniquely determined by the rooted forest $\mathcal{F}_{(r)}(\widetilde{T}),$
as illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:Tnl_structure}.
Let $S=\{i_{1},i_{2},\ldots,i_{s}\}$
be the set of roots of the connected components of the forest
$\mathcal{F}_{(r)}(\widetilde{T})$.
Then $\emptyset\neq S\subseteq\{2,3,\ldots,n-\ell\}.$
Therefore, the set $S$ with $s$ elements can be chosen in
$\binom{n-\ell-1}{s}$
ways.
Now fix such a subset $S$ with $|S|=s$. By Theorem~\ref{Thm:forest}, the number of rooted forests with roots specified by the vertices of $S$ is
$s(n-2)^{n-3-s}.$
Hence,
\begin{equation*}\begin{split}|\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}|&=\sum_{s=1}^{n-\ell-1}\binom{n-\ell-1}{s}s(n-2)^{n-3-s}\\
&=\sum_{s=1}^{n-\ell-1}(n-\ell-1)\binom{n-\ell-2}{s-1}(n-2)^{n-3-s}\\
&\hskip 250.38434pt(\text{By setting }s-1=j)\\
&=(n-\ell-1)\sum_{j=0}^{n-\ell-2}\binom{n-\ell-2}{j}(n-2)^{n-4-j}\\
&=(n-\ell-1)~(n-2)^{\ell-2}\left(\sum_{j=0}^{n-\ell-2}\binom{n-\ell-2}{j}(n-2)^{n-\ell-2-j}\right)\\
&=(n-\ell-1)~(n-2)^{\ell-2}\bigl((n-2)+1\bigr)^{n-\ell-2}\\
&=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}~(n-2)^{\ell-2}~(n-\ell-1).\end{split}\end{equation*}
This completes the proof.
\qed\end@proof
\par Throughout this section, the phrase \emph{attach a rooted forest to a vertex} means attaching each root of the connected components of the forest to that vertex.
Likewise, \emph{attach a rooted subtree to a vertex} means attaching the root of the subtree to that vertex.
\par\begin{proposition}For $n\geq\ell+2$ and $\ell\geq 2$,
there exists a bijection $\psi\colon\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$.
Thus, we deduce that
$$|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}|=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}~(n-2)^{\ell-2}~(n-\ell-1).$$\end{proposition}
\par\par\@proof Let $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$. Consider the unique path $Q$ in $\widetilde{T}$ from the root $r=n-\ell+2$ to the vertex $1$.
Suppose that $Q$ is of the form
$$Q\colon\quad r\to b_{1}\to b_{2}\to\cdots\to b_{\beta}\to 1,\qquad(\beta\geq 1).$$
Since all the sons of the root other than the leaf $n-\ell+1$ belong to $\{2,3,\ldots,n-\ell\}$, we have $b_{1}\notin F_{\ell}.$
Let $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)$ be the set of sons of $1$ in $\widetilde{T}$, and $\operatorname{par}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)$ be the parent of $1$ in $\widetilde{T}$.
Clearly, $\operatorname{par}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)=b_{\beta}$.
\par We now consider the following cases.
In each of the cases, we also describe the reverse construction, thereby showing that the corresponding transformations are reversible.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\par\noindent$\textbf{Case I.}$
Suppose that in $\widetilde{T}$, the vertex $1$ is not adjacent to any vertex in $F_{\ell}$.
That is, $b_{\beta}=\operatorname{par}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\notin F_{\ell}$ and $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}=\emptyset.$
Hence, we have $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}.$
In this case, we define $\psi(\widetilde{T})=\widetilde{T}.$
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent Conversely, if $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$, then $\operatorname{root}(T)=r=n-\ell+2$, and the vertex $1$ is not adjacent to any vertex in $F_{\ell}$.
Moreover, if the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)$ in $F_{\ell}$ is of the form $r=n-\ell+2\to n-\ell+1,$ with $n-\ell+1$ a leaf of $T$, then $T\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$ satisfying Case~I, and $\psi(T)=T.$
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent$\textbf{Case II.}$
Suppose that $b_{\beta}=\operatorname{par}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\notin F_{\ell}$, but $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}\neq\emptyset$.
In this case, consider the tree $\widetilde{T}^{\prime}=\widetilde{T}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T})\to(n-\ell+1)\bigr]$
obtained by attaching descendants of $1$ to the leaf $n-\ell+1$ in $\widetilde{T}$.
Then $1$ becomes a leaf of $\widetilde{T}^{\prime}$, and $\widetilde{T}^{\prime}$ satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}.
Applying Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}, we obtain a tree $(\widetilde{T}^{\prime})^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$, and define $\psi(\widetilde{T})=(\widetilde{T}^{\prime})^{*}$.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent We now identify the trees in $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$ arising from trees in $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$ satisfying Case~II.
Let $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$ whose maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)$ has the form
\begin{equation}r=n-\ell+2\to\mathrel{\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}\rotatebox{90.0}{$=$}\\
n-\ell+1\end{subarray}}{a_{1}}}\to a_{2}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha},\qquad(\alpha>1),\end{equation}
where $a_{\alpha}$ is a leaf of $T$, and $1$ is not a descendant of $a_{1}=n-\ell+1$.
Consider the trees $T^{\prime}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(T)\to a_{\alpha}\bigr]$,
obtained by attaching descendants of $1$ to the leaf $a_{\alpha}$ in $T$, and $T^{2}=T^{\prime}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{a_{1}}(T^{\prime})\to 1\bigr]$,
obtained by attaching all descendants of $a_{1}=n-\ell+1$ to the leaf $1$ in $T^{\prime}$.
Clearly, $T^{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$, and $T^{2}=\widetilde{T}$ is a tree satisfying the conditions of Case~II.
Reversing the above constructions, we obtain $\psi(\widetilde{T})=\psi(T^{2})=T$.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent$\textbf{Case III.}$
Suppose that $b_{\beta}=\operatorname{par}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\in F_{\ell}.$
Since $b_{1}\notin F_{\ell}$, we necessarily have $\beta>1$.
Interchanging the vertices $b_{1}$ and $b_{\beta}$ in $\widetilde{T}$, we obtain a tree, denoted by $\widetilde{T}^{2}$.
Under this interchange, the path $Q$ becomes a path $Q^{\prime}$ of the form
\begin{equation}Q^{\prime}:\quad r\to b_{1}^{\prime}\to b_{2}^{\prime}\to\cdots\to b_{\beta}^{\prime}\to 1,\end{equation}
where $b_{1}^{\prime}=b_{\beta}$, $b_{\beta}^{\prime}=b_{1}$, and $b_{j}^{\prime}=b_{j}$, for all $j\notin\{1,\beta\}$.
For each $b_{j}^{\prime}$ on the path $Q^{\prime}$, let $\mathcal{F}_{(b_{j}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ denote the rooted forest consisting of all descendants of $b_{j}^{\prime}$ in $\widetilde{T}^{2}$ excluding those lying on the path $Q^{\prime}$.
Clearly, $\mathcal{F}_{(b_{j}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})=\mathcal{F}_{(b_{j})}(\widetilde{T})$ for all $j$.
Suppose that $b_{1}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\ldots,b_{t}^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}$, but $b_{t+1}^{\prime}\notin F_{\ell}$.
Since $b_{\beta}^{\prime}=b_{1}\notin F_{\ell}$, we have $t<\beta$.
The path $Q^{\prime}$ in $\widetilde{T}^{2}$ together with its descendants is illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:caseIII_structure}.

\par\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent We now divide Case~III into two subcases.
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent$\textbf{Subcase III(a).}$
Suppose that for every $1\leq j\leq t$, all sons of $b_{j}^{\prime}$ in the rooted forest $\mathcal{F}_{(b_{j}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ are smaller than $b_{j}^{\prime}$.
Let $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{b_{1}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{2})=b_{1}^{\prime}\vee\mathcal{F}_{b_{1}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ denote the rooted subtree of $\widetilde{T}^{2}$ consisting of $b_{1}^{\prime}$ together with all of its descendants, rooted at $b_{1}^{\prime}$.
Consider the tree $\widetilde{T}^{3}$ obtained from $\widetilde{T}^{2}$, by removing the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{b_{1}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ and attaching it to $n-\ell+1$, as illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:T3_structure}.

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{12}}{Illustration of the tree $\widetilde{T}^{3}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 12}}{\small Illustration of the tree $\widetilde{T}^{3}$.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\noindent\par Next, remove the forests $\mathcal{F}_{(b_{j}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ for $1\leq j\leq t$ from $\widetilde{T}^{3}$.
Then attach $\mathcal{F}_{(b_{1}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ to $n-\ell+1$, and, for each $1<j\leq t$, attach $\mathcal{F}_{(b_{j}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ to $b_{j-1}^{\prime}$, thereby obtaining $\widetilde{T}^{4}$ (see Figure~\ref{fig:T4_structure}).

\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{13}}{The tree $\widetilde{T}^{4}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 13}}{\small The tree $\widetilde{T}^{4}$.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\par\noindent Let $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{b_{t+1}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{4})=b_{t+1}^{\prime}\vee\mathcal{F}_{b_{t+1}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{4})$ denote the rooted subtree consisting of the vertex $b_{t+1}^{\prime}$ together with all of its descendants.
Observe that $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{b_{t+1}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{4})=\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{b_{t+1}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$.
By removing $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{b_{t+1}^{\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{4})$ and attaching it to $b_{t-1}^{\prime}$ in $\widetilde{T}^{4}$, we obtain $\widetilde{T}^{5}$, as illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:T5_structure}.

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{(b)}}{The rooted subtree
$\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{(b_{t+1}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\small(b)}}{\small The rooted subtree
$\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{(b_{t+1}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$.}}}\@add@centering\end{subfigure}\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{14}}{The structure of the tree $\widetilde{T}^{5}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 14}}{\small The structure of the tree $\widetilde{T}^{5}$.}}}
\par\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\par\par\par\noindent If $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}=\emptyset$, then $\widetilde{T}^{5}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$, and we define $\psi(\widetilde{T})=\widetilde{T}^{5}$.
Otherwise, suppose that $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}\neq\emptyset$.
In this case, we consider $\widetilde{T}^{6}=\widetilde{T}^{5}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T}^{2})\to b_{t}^{\prime}\bigr]$,
obtained by attaching descendants of $1$ to the leaf $b_{t}^{\prime}$ in $\widetilde{T}^{5}$.
Clearly, $\widetilde{T}^{6}$ satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}.
Thus, by applying Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}, we obtain a tree $(\widetilde{T}^{6})^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$, and we define $\psi(\widetilde{T})=(\widetilde{T}^{6})^{*}$.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent$\textbf{Subcase III(b).}$
Let $p\in[t]=\{1,2,\dots,t\}$ be the least integer such that the largest son of $b_{p}^{\prime}$ in $\mathcal{F}_{(b_{p}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ is a vertex $c>b_{p}^{\prime}$.
Proceeding as in Subcase~III(a), we construct $\widetilde{T}^{3}$.
The rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{(b_{p}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})=b_{p}^{\prime}\vee\mathcal{F}_{(b_{p}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ has the form illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:subcaseIIIb_structure}.

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{15}}{Illustration of the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{(b_{p}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})=b_{p}^{\prime}\vee\mathcal{F}_{(b_{p}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 15}}{\small Illustration of the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{(b_{p}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})=b_{p}^{\prime}\vee\mathcal{F}_{(b_{p}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\noindent In $\widetilde{T}^{3}$, we now remove the forests $\mathcal{F}_{(b_{j}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ for $1\leq j\leq p$.
Then attach $\mathcal{F}_{(b_{1}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ to $n-\ell+1$, and, for each $1<j\leq p$, attach $\mathcal{F}_{(b_{j}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ to $b_{j-1}^{\prime}$.
Finally, remove $\mathcal{F}_{c}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ and attach it to $b_{p}^{\prime}$, thereby obtaining $\widetilde{T}^{7}$ (see Figure~\ref{fig:T7_structure}).

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{(b)}}{The tree $\widetilde{T}(b_{p}^{\prime})$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\small(b)}}{\small The tree $\widetilde{T}(b_{p}^{\prime})$.}}}\@add@centering\end{subfigure}\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{16}}{The structure of the tree $\widetilde{T}^{7}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 16}}{\small The structure of the tree $\widetilde{T}^{7}$.}}}
\par\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\par\noindent Observe that $\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T}^{7})=\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$.
If $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}^{7}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}=\emptyset$, then $\widetilde{T}^{7}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$, and we define $\psi(\widetilde{T})=\widetilde{T}^{7}$.
Otherwise, if $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}^{7}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}\neq\emptyset$, then we consider the tree $\widetilde{T}^{8}=\widetilde{T}^{7}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T}^{7})\to c\bigr],$
obtained by attaching descendants of $1$ in $\widetilde{T}^{7}$ to the leaf $c$.
Since $\widetilde{T}^{8}$ satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}, we obtain $(\widetilde{T}^{8})^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$.
In this case, we define $\psi(\widetilde{T})=(\widetilde{T}^{8})^{*}$.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent We now characterize the trees $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$ that arise from Case~III.
Let $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$, and let $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)$
$$r=n-\ell+2=a_{0}\to a_{1}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha},$$
be the maximum path of $T$ in $F_{\ell}$ as in~\eqref{eq:Pmax_path}.
Suppose that $1$ is a descendant of $a_{t}$ for some $1\leq t<\alpha$ but is not a descendant of $a_{t+1}$.
Since $1$ is a descendant of $a_{t}$, there exists a unique path $Q^{\prime}$ of the form
\begin{equation}Q^{\prime}:\quad a_{t}=a_{0}^{\prime}\to a_{1}^{\prime}\to\cdots\to a_{q}^{\prime}\to 1,\qquad(q\geq 1),\end{equation}
in $T$.
Recall that, for the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)$, $\mathcal{F}_{a_{i}}(T)$ denotes the rooted forest consisting of all descendants of $a_{i}$ in $T$. Then $\mathcal{F}_{(a_{i})}(T)$ denotes the rooted forest obtained from $\mathcal{F}_{a_{i}}(T)$ by removing the subtree rooted at $a_{i+1}$.
We divide the analysis into two subclasses.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{Subclass (1).}
Suppose that the son of $a_{t}$ lying on the path $Q^{\prime}$ does not belong to $F_{\ell}$, i.e., $a_{1}^{\prime}\notin F_{\ell}$.
Then we consider the tree $T^{3}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(T)\to a_{\alpha}\bigr],$
obtained by attaching descendants of $1$ to the leaf $a_{\alpha}$ in $T$.
Suppose that the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{(a_{t})}(T^{3})=a_{t}\vee\mathcal{F}_{(a_{t})}(T^{3})$ has the form illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:subclass1_structure}.

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{17}}{Structure of the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{(a_{t})}(T^{3})$ in $T^{3}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 17}}{\small Structure of the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{(a_{t})}(T^{3})$ in $T^{3}$.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\par\par\noindent Let $\mathcal{F}_{a_{t+1}}(T^{3})$ denote the rooted forest consisting of all descendants of $a_{t+1}$ in $T^{3}$.
Now consider the tree $T^{4}=T^{3}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{a_{t+1}}(T^{3})\to 1\bigr],$
obtained by attaching all descendants of $a_{t+1}$ to the leaf $1$ in $T^{3}$.
The tree $T^{4}$ has the form as shown in Figure~\ref{fig:T4_caseIII}.

\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{(b)}}{The rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{(a_{1}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{3})$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\small(b)}}{\small The rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{(a_{1}^{\prime})}(\widetilde{T}^{3})$.}}}\@add@centering\end{subfigure}\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{18}}{Illustration of the structure of $T^{4}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 18}}{\small Illustration of the structure of $T^{4}$.}}}
\par\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\par\par\noindent\par Next, we remove the forests $\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{(a_{t})}(T^{3})$, $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{1}^{\prime}}(T^{3})$, and $\mathcal{F}_{(a_{i})}(T^{3})$ for $1\leq i<t$ from $T^{4}$. Then attach $\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{(a_{t})}(T^{3})$ and $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{1}^{\prime}}(T^{3})$ to $a_{t+1}$.
Then, for each $1\leq i<t$, attach $\mathcal{F}_{(a_{i})}(T^{3})$ to $a_{i+1}$. The resulting tree $T^{5}$ is illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:T5_caseIII}.

\par\par\noindent Now, consider the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{2}}(T^{5}),$ consisting of the vertex $a_{2}$ together with all its descendants in $T^{5}$.
By removing $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{2}}(T^{5})$ from $T^{5}$ and attaching it to the root $r=n-\ell+2$ in $T^{5}$, we obtain $T^{6}$, illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:T6_caseIII}.

\par\par\noindent Finally, by interchanging the vertices $a_{2}$ and $a_{q}^{\prime}$ in $T^{6}$, we obtain a tree $T^{7}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$.
Reversing the above constructions, we see that $\psi(T^{7})=T$.
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{Subclass (2).}
Suppose that the son of $a_{t}=a_{0}^{\prime}$ lying on the path $Q^{\prime}$ belongs to $F_{\ell}$; that is, $a_{1}^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}$.
Proceeding as in Subclass~(1), we first construct the tree $T^{4}$. From $T^{4}$, we then construct a tree $T^{8}$ of the form illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:T8_caseIII}.

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{21}}{The tree $T^{8}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 21}}{\small The tree $T^{8}$.}}}
\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\noindent Let $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{2}}(T^{8})$ denote the rooted subtree consisting of the vertex $a_{2}$ together with all its descendants in $T^{8}$. By removing $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{2}}(T^{8})$ from $T^{8}$ and attaching it to the root $r=n-\ell+2$, we obtain a tree $T^{9}$ from $T^{8}$.
Let $T^{10}$ be the tree obtained from $T^{9}$ by interchanging the vertices $a_{2}$ and $a_{q}^{\prime}$.
Clearly, $T^{10}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$.
Reversing the above constructions, we obtain $\psi(T^{10})=T.$
\par\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent This shows that the map $\psi\colon\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$ is well-defined and surjective.
We now briefly characterize the trees $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$ arising in the above three cases.
Let $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)\colon n-\ell+2=a_{0}\to a_{1}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha}$ be the maximum path of $T$ in $F_{\ell}$ as in \eqref{eq:Pmax_path}.
\par\begin{enumerate}\enumerate@item@If $\alpha=1$, then $T$ arises from Case~I.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@If $\alpha>1$ and $1$ is a descendant of $a_{1}=n-\ell+1$, then $T$ arises from Case~II.
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\enumerate@item@Suppose $\alpha>1$ and there exists $1\leq t<\alpha$ such that $1$ is a descendant of $a_{t}$ but not of $a_{t+1}$, then $T$ arises from Case~III.
Let $Q^{\prime}\colon a_{t}=a_{0}^{\prime}\to a_{1}^{\prime}\to\cdots\to a_{q}^{\prime}\to 1$ be the unique path from $a_{t}$ to $1$.
Then:
\begin{itemize}\itemize@item@if $a_{1}^{\prime}\notin F_{\ell}$, then $T$ arises from
Subcase~III(a),
\par\vskip 4.0pt\vskip 4.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 1.0pt\itemize@item@if $a_{1}^{\prime}\in F_{\ell}$, then $T$ arises from
Subcase~III(b).
\end{itemize}\end{enumerate}
\par Since the cases are mutually exclusive and exhaustive, and each
$T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$ arises from exactly one $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}$,
the map $\psi$ is a bijection.
Therefore, $|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}|=|\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell}|$, and the required formula follows from Proposition~\ref{prop:Tnl_count}.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par Proposition~\ref{prop:Tnl_bijection} determines the cardinality of
$\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}$.
We now turn to the enumeration of $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ for $0\leq p<\ell-2$.
For this purpose, we consider the sets $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ and $\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}$.
\par By Proposition~\ref{prop:Cnlp_characterization}, a tree $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ if and only if $T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell-1}}$ and conditions \ref{cond1} and \ref{cond2} are satisfied with
$F_{\ell-1}=\{n-\ell+2,n-\ell+3,\ldots,n\}$.
In particular, the vertex $1$ is not adjacent to any vertex of $F_{\ell-1}$, but it is still possible that $1$ is adjacent to the vertex $n-\ell+1$ in $T$.
Now, we define the subset
$$\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}=\{T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}:\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\notin F_{\ell}\}.$$
Since $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ implies $\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\notin F_{\ell-1}$, the only possibility excluded by the condition $\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\notin F_{\ell}$ is $\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)=n-\ell+1$.
Thus, $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ consists precisely of those trees $T$ for which $\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\neq n-\ell+1$.
Note that the vertex $n-\ell+1$ may still be a son of $1$ in such a tree.
\par\par Similarly, a tree $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}$ if and only if $T\in\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{p+2}}$ and conditions \ref{cond1} and \ref{cond2} of Proposition~\ref{prop:Cnlp_characterization} hold, with $F_{p+2}=\{n-p-1,n-p,\ldots,n\}$.
So, $\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\notin F_{p+2}$.
We define
$$\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}=\{T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}:\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\notin F_{\ell}\}.$$
\par For each $T\in\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, let $\widetilde{T}$ be the tree obtained from $T$ by interchanging the vertices $n-p-1$ and $n-\ell+1$ in $T$.
We define
$$\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}=\{\widetilde{T}:T\in\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}\}.$$
\par Since $T\mapsto\widetilde{T}$ is a bijection from $\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}$ to $\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, we have $|\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}|$.
Moreover, $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}=\emptyset$.
Indeed, in any tree in $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$, the vertex $n-\ell+1$ is never a son of the root $n-p$.
On the other hand, in every tree $\widetilde{T}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, the vertex $n-\ell+1$ is a son of the root $n-p$.
This follows from the fact that $\widetilde{T}$ is obtained by interchanging the vertices $n-p-1$ and $n-\ell+1$ in a tree of $\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, where $n-p-1$ is the largest son of the root $n-p$. Therefore, no tree can belong to both $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ and $\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$.
\par Now, we shall see that these subsets play a key role in the enumeration of $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
\par\begin{proposition}For $0\leq p<\ell-2$, there exists a bijection
$$\Phi_{p}\colon\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}\coprod\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}.$$Consequently, $|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}|=|\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|+|\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}|.$
\end{proposition}
\par\par\@proof We define the map $\Phi_{p}$ separately on the two disjoint subsets $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ and $\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, and characterize the image of each part together with the corresponding inverse construction.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{The map on $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$.}
Let $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$.
Suppose that the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell-1}(\widetilde{T})$ of $\widetilde{T}$ in $F_{\ell-1}$ is of the form
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell-1}(\widetilde{T})\colon\quad r=n-p\to a_{1}^{\prime}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha-1}^{\prime}\to a_{\alpha}^{\prime},\qquad(\alpha\geq 1),$$
where $a_{i}^{\prime}\in F_{\ell-1}$.
Since $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$, we have $\operatorname{par}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\notin F_{\ell}$.
\par If $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}=\emptyset$, then $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$, and we define $\Phi_{p}(\widetilde{T})=\widetilde{T}$.
Now suppose that $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}\neq\emptyset$.
That is, $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}=\{n-\ell+1\}$.
Consider the tree $\widetilde{T}^{\prime}=\widetilde{T}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T})\to a_{\alpha}^{\prime}\bigr],$
obtained by attaching the descendants of $1$ to the leaf $a_{\alpha}^{\prime}$ in $\widetilde{T}$.
Clearly, $\widetilde{T}^{\prime}$ satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}.
Moreover, the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T}^{\prime})$ in $F_{\ell}$ is of the form
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T}^{\prime})\colon\quad r=n-p\to a_{1}^{\prime}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha}^{\prime}\to a_{\alpha+1}^{\prime}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha+t}^{\prime},\qquad(t\geq 1),$$
where $a_{\alpha+1}^{\prime}=n-\ell+1$.
Applying Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}, we obtain a tree $(\widetilde{T}^{\prime})^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
We define $\Phi_{p}(\widetilde{T})=(\widetilde{T}^{\prime})^{*}$.
\par In fact, we shall show that $\Phi_{p}(\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p})$ consists precisely of those trees $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ such that the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)$ is of the form
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)\colon\quad r=n-p\to a_{1}\to a_{2}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha-1}\to a_{\alpha},\qquad(\alpha\geq 1),$$
where $a_{i}\in F_{\ell}$ for all $1\leq i\leq\alpha$, $a_{\alpha}$ is a leaf and $n-\ell+1<a_{1}<r=n-p$.
Moreover, either $a_{i}\in F_{\ell-1}$ for all $1\leq i\leq\alpha$, or there exists an index $t$ with $1<t\leq\alpha$ such that $a_{t}=n-\ell+1$ and the vertex $1$ is not a descendant of $\operatorname{par}_{T}(a_{t})$ in $T$.
\par In the former case, we clearly have $T\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ and $\Phi_{p}(T)=T.$
In the latter case, consider the tree $T^{\prime}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(T)\to a_{\alpha}\bigr],$
obtained by attaching all descendants of $1$ to the leaf $a_{\alpha}$ in $T$.
Let $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{t}}(T^{\prime})=a_{t}\vee\mathcal{F}_{a_{t}}(T^{\prime})$ denote the rooted subtree consisting of the vertex $a_{t}$ together with all its descendants in $T^{\prime}$.
We then construct a tree $T^{2}$ by removing $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{t}}(T^{\prime})$ from $T^{\prime}$ and attaching it to the leaf $1$.
By construction, $T^{2}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$, and $\operatorname{son}_{T^{2}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}=\{n-\ell+1\}$.
Reversing the above constructions, we recover $T$ from $T^{2}$, and therefore $\Phi_{p}(T^{2})=T$.
\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\par\par\noindent{The map on $\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$.}
Let $\widetilde{T}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$.
Recall that every tree in $\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$ is obtained from a tree in $\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}$ by interchanging the vertices $n-p-1$ and $n-\ell+1$.
Let
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{p+2}(T)\colon\quad r=n-p\to\widetilde{a}_{1}\to\widetilde{a}_{2}\to\cdots\to\widetilde{a}_{t},\qquad(t\geq 1),$$
be the maximum path of the corresponding tree $T\in\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}$ in $F_{p+2}$, where $\widetilde{a}_{i}\in F_{p+2}$, $\widetilde{a}_{1}=n-p-1$, and $\widetilde{a}_{t}$ is a leaf.
By abuse of notation, we write
\begin{equation}\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{p+2}(\widetilde{T})\colon\quad r=n-p\to a_{1}^{\prime\prime}\to a_{2}^{\prime\prime}\to\cdots\to a_{t}^{\prime\prime},\end{equation}
where $a_{1}^{\prime\prime}=n-\ell+1$, $a_{j}^{\prime\prime}=\widetilde{a}_{j}\in F_{p+2}\setminus\{n-p-1\}$ $(2\leq j\leq t)$, and $a_{t}^{\prime\prime}=\widetilde{a}_{t}$ is a leaf of $\widetilde{T}$.
Note that $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{p+2}(\widetilde{T})$ as defined in~\eqref{eq:path_p+2} need not be the maximum path of $\widetilde{T}$ in $F_{p+2}$.
However, throughout this subsection, the notation $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{p+2}(\widetilde{T})$ will always refer to the path obtained from $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{p+2}(T)$ by interchanging the vertices $n-p-1$ and $n-\ell+1$.
We consider the following cases.
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{Case (i).}
Suppose that the vertex $1$ is not adjacent to any vertex of $F_{\ell}$ in $\widetilde{T}$, and that $n-\ell+1$ is the largest son of the root $r=n-p$.
Then $\widetilde{T}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$, and we define $\Phi_{p}(\widetilde{T})=\widetilde{T}$.
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{Case (ii).}
Suppose that $n-\ell+1$ is the largest son of the root $r=n-p$ in $\widetilde{T}$, but $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}\neq\emptyset$.
Recall that, by abuse of notation, $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{p+2}(\widetilde{T})$ denotes the path obtained from the maximum path of the corresponding tree in $\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}$.
In the present case, since $n-\ell+1$ is the largest son of the root $r=n-p$ in $\widetilde{T}$, this path is in fact the maximum path of $\widetilde{T}$ in $F_{p+2}$.
Thus,
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{p+2}(\widetilde{T})\colon\quad r=n-p\to a_{1}^{\prime\prime}\to a_{2}^{\prime\prime}\to\cdots\to a_{t}^{\prime\prime},\qquad(t\geq 1),$$
where $a_{1}^{\prime\prime}=n-\ell+1$, $a_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in F_{p+2}\setminus\{n-p-1\}$ for $2\leq i\leq t$, and $a_{t}^{\prime\prime}$ is a leaf of $\widetilde{T}$.
Consider the tree $\widetilde{T}^{2}=\widetilde{T}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T})\to a_{t}^{\prime\prime}\bigr]$
obtained by attaching all descendants of $1$ to the leaf $a_{t}^{\prime\prime}$ in $\widetilde{T}$.
Then the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ in $F_{\ell}$ has the form
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T}^{2})\colon\quad r=n-p\to a_{1}^{\prime\prime}\to a_{2}^{\prime\prime}\to\cdots\to a_{t}^{\prime\prime}\to a_{t+1}^{\prime\prime}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime},\qquad(\alpha>t).$$
Clearly, the tree $\widetilde{T}^{2}$ satisfies the hypotheses of
Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}, and applying it yields a tree $(\widetilde{T}^{2})^{*}=\widetilde{T}^{2}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{a_{\alpha}^{\prime\prime}}(\widetilde{T}^{2})\to 1\bigr]\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}.$
Here, we define $\Phi_{p}(\widetilde{T})=(\widetilde{T}^{2})^{*}$.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\par We now identify the trees in $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ arising from Case~(ii).
Let $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ whose maximum path
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)\colon\quad r=n-p\to a_{1}\to a_{2}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha},\qquad(a_{i}\in F_{\ell}),$$
contains an index $t$ with $1<t<\alpha$ such that $a_{1}=n-\ell+1$,
$a_{i}\in F_{p+2}\setminus\{n-p-1\}$ for $1<i\leq t$,
$a_{t+1}\notin F_{p+2}\setminus\{n-p-1\}$, and
vertex $1$ is not a descendant of $a_{t}$.
Consider the tree
$$T^{3}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(T)\to a_{\alpha}\bigr],$$
obtained by attaching all descendants of $1$ to the leaf $a_{\alpha}$ in $T$.
Next, consider the tree
$$T^{4}=T^{3}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{a_{t+1}}(T^{3})\to 1\bigr].$$
Then $T^{4}\in\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, and reversing the above construction gives $\Phi_{p}(T^{4})=T$.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{Case (iii).}
Suppose that the largest son of the root $r=n-p$ in $\widetilde{T}$ is a vertex $b\neq n-\ell+1$.
Since $\widetilde{T}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$ is obtained from a tree in $\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}$ by interchanging the vertices $n-p-1$ and $n-\ell+1$, it follows that $n-\ell+1<b<n-p$.
Let $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T})$ be the maximum path of $\widetilde{T}$ in $F_{\ell}$.
Then
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T})\colon\quad r=n-p\to b=b_{1}\to b_{2}\to\cdots\to b_{\beta-1}\to b_{\beta},\qquad(\beta\geq 1),$$
where $b_{i}\in F_{\ell}$ for all $1\leq i\leq\beta$.
We divide the analysis into the following three subcases.
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{Subcase (iiia).}
Suppose that the vertex $1$ is a descendant of $b_{\beta}$ in $\widetilde{T}$.
Recall that
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{p+2}(\widetilde{T})\colon\quad r=n-p\to a_{1}^{\prime\prime}\to a_{2}^{\prime\prime}\to\cdots\to a_{t}^{\prime\prime},$$
where $a_{1}^{\prime\prime}=n-\ell+1$, $a_{i}^{\prime\prime}\in F_{p+2}\setminus\{n-p-1\}$ and $a_{t}^{\prime\prime}$ is a leaf.
First, construct the tree $\widetilde{T}^{2}=\widetilde{T}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T})\to a_{t}^{\prime\prime}\bigr]$, by attaching the descendants of $1$ to the leaf $a_{t}^{\prime\prime}$ in $\widetilde{T}$.
Let $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n-\ell+1}(\widetilde{T}^{2})=(n-\ell+1)\vee\mathcal{F}_{n-\ell+1}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ denote the rooted subtree consisting of the vertex $n-\ell+1$ together with all its descendants in $\widetilde{T}^{2}$.
We obtain a tree $\widetilde{T}^{3}$ by removing $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n-\ell+1}(\widetilde{T}^{2})$ from $\widetilde{T}^{2}$ and attaching it to the vertex $b_{\beta}$.
Suppose that the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T}^{3})$ of $\widetilde{T}^{3}$ in $F_{\ell}$ is of the form
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T}^{3})\colon\quad r=n-p\to b_{1}\to b_{2}\to\cdots\to b_{\beta}\to b_{\beta+1}\to\cdots\to b_{\beta+t},\qquad(t\geq 1),$$
where $b_{\beta+1}=n-\ell+1$.
Then $\widetilde{T}^{3}$ satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}.
By applying Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}, we obtain the tree $(\widetilde{T}^{3})^{*}=\widetilde{T}^{3}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{(b_{\beta+t})}(\widetilde{T}^{3})\to 1\bigr]\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
We define $\Phi_{p}(\widetilde{T})=(\widetilde{T}^{3})^{*}$.
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{Subcase (iiib).}
Suppose that the vertex $1$ is a descendant of $n-\ell+1$ in $\widetilde{T}$. Proceeding exactly as in Subcase~(iiia), we construct the trees $\widetilde{T}^{2}$, $\widetilde{T}^{3}$ and obtain a tree $(\widetilde{T}^{3})^{*}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
We then define $\Phi_{p}(\widetilde{T})=(\widetilde{T}^{3})^{*}$.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0ptThe images arising from Subcases~(iiia) and~(iiib) can be distinguished by the position of the vertex $1$.
In Subcase~(iiia), the vertex $1$ is a descendant of $b_{\beta}$ but not of $n-\ell+1$ in $\Phi_{p}(\widetilde{T})$.
On the other hand, in Subcase~(iiib), the vertex $1$ is a descendant of $n-\ell+1$ in $\Phi_{p}(\widetilde{T})$.
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{Subcase (iiic).}
Suppose that the vertex $1$ is neither a descendant of $n-\ell+1$ nor a descendant of $b_{\beta}$ in $\widetilde{T}$.
Consider the rooted forest $\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T})$ consisting of all descendants of $1$ in $\widetilde{T}$, and let
$r\vee\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T})$
be the rooted tree obtained by attaching $\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T})$ to the root $r=n-p$.
Suppose that the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(r\vee\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T}))$ of $r\vee\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T})$ in $F_{\ell}$ is
$$r=n-p=c_{0}\to c_{1}\to c_{2}\to\cdots\to c_{\gamma},\qquad(\gamma\geq 0),$$
where $c_{i}\in F_{\ell}$ $(0\leq i\leq\gamma)$.
Note that if $1$ is a leaf of $\widetilde{T}$, or more generally if $\operatorname{son}_{\widetilde{T}}(1)\cap F_{\ell}=\emptyset$, then $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(r\vee\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T}))=\{r\}$, that is, $\gamma=0$.
We construct a tree $\widetilde{T}^{4}$ from $\widetilde{T}$ by removing the forest $\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T})$ from vertex $1$ and attaching it to the vertex $r$.
Let $\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{(r)}(\widetilde{T}^{4})$ denote the rooted forest obtained from the descendants of the root $r$ in $\widetilde{T}^{4}$ by removing the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n-\ell+1}(\widetilde{T}^{4})$ and the rooted forest $\mathcal{F}_{1}(\widetilde{T})$,
which is now attached to $r$.
The structure of $\widetilde{T}^{4}$ is illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:subcaseIIIC_T4}.

{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{3.0pt,3.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{{}{}{{}}}{{}{}{{}}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 20.71 8.23 L -108.4 8.23 C -111.45 8.23 -113.93 5.75 -113.93 2.7 L -113.93 -250.9 C -113.93 -253.96 -111.45 -256.43 -108.4 -256.43 L 20.71 -256.43 C 23.77 -256.43 26.25 -253.96 26.25 -250.9 L 26.25 2.7 C 26.25 5.75 23.77 8.23 20.71 8.23 Z M -113.93 -256.43}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope }{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-65.26636pt}{-89.6875pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\par{{}{}{}}{{}}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{36.92807pt}{-91.93169pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{63}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\par
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{(c)}}{The rooted tree $r\vee\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{(r)}(\widetilde{T}^{4})$ where $1$ is a leaf which is not a descendant of $b_{\beta}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{\small(c)}}{\small The rooted tree $r\vee\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{(r)}(\widetilde{T}^{4})$ where $1$ is a leaf which is not a descendant of $b_{\beta}$.}}}\@add@centering\end{subfigure}\par\@@toccaption{{\lx@tag[ ]{{22}}{Structure of the tree $\widetilde{T}^{4}$.}}}\@@caption{{\lx@tag[. ]{{\small Figure 22}}{\small Structure of the tree $\widetilde{T}^{4}$.}}}
\par\@add@centering\end{figure}
\par\par\noindent Next, we construct the rooted tree $\widetilde{T}^{5}$ from $\widetilde{T}^{4}$ by removing the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{n-\ell+1}(\widetilde{T}^{4})=(n-\ell+1)\vee\mathcal{F}_{n-\ell+1}(\widetilde{T}^{4})$ and attaching it to the vertex $c_{\gamma}$ in $\widetilde{T}^{4}$.
Suppose that the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T}^{5})$ of $\widetilde{T}^{5}$ in $F_{\ell}$ is of the form
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T}^{5})\colon\quad r=n-p\to c_{1}\to\cdots\to c_{\gamma}\to c_{\gamma+1}\to\cdots\to c_{\gamma+t},$$
where $c_{\gamma+1}=n-\ell+1$, $c_{\gamma+i}\in F_{p+2}\setminus\{n-p-1\}$ $(1<i\leq t)$ and $c_{\gamma+t}$ is a leaf.
Observe that $\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{(r)}(\widetilde{T}^{5})=\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{(r)}(\widetilde{T}^{4})$.
We then construct $\widetilde{T}^{6}$ from $\widetilde{T}^{5}$ by removing $\mathcal{F}^{\prime}_{(r)}(\widetilde{T}^{5})$ from $\widetilde{T}^{5}$ and attaching it to the leaf $c_{\gamma+t}$.
The tree $\widetilde{T}^{6}$ satisfies the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp} with the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T}^{6})$ of $\widetilde{T}^{6}$ in $F_{\ell}$ of the form
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(\widetilde{T}^{6})\colon\quad r=n-p\to c_{1}\to\cdots\to c_{\gamma}\to c_{\gamma+1}\to\cdots\to c_{\gamma+t}\to c_{\gamma+t+1}\to\cdots\to c_{\gamma+t+\beta},$$
where $c_{\gamma+1}=n-\ell+1$,
$c_{\gamma+i}\in F_{p+2}\setminus\{n-p-1\}$ $(1<i\leq t)$,
$c_{\gamma+t+1}=b\in F_{\ell}\setminus F_{p+2}$, and
$c_{\gamma+t+j}\in F_{\ell}$ $(1\leq j\leq\beta)$.
Therefore, by Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}, we obtain $(\widetilde{T}^{6})^{*}=\widetilde{T}^{6}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{c_{\gamma+t+\beta}}(\widetilde{T}^{6})\to 1\bigr]\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
We define $\Phi_{p}(\widetilde{T})=(\widetilde{T}^{6})^{*}$.
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\par We now characterize the trees $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ that arise from Case~(iii).
Suppose that the maximum path $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)$ of $T$ in $F_{\ell}$ is of the form
$$\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell}(T)\colon\quad r=n-p\to a_{1}\to a_{2}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha-1}\to a_{\alpha},\qquad(\alpha>1),$$
with $a_{s}=n-\ell+1$ for some $1<s\leq\alpha$.
If $s=\alpha$ or $a_{s+1}\notin F_{p+2}$, we set $t=0$.
Otherwise, let $t$ be the largest positive integer such that $s+t\leq\alpha$ and $a_{s+j}\in F_{p+2}$ for $1\leq j\leq t$.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0ptIf the vertex $1$ is a descendant of $a_{s-1}$ but not of $a_{s}$ in $T$, then $T$ arises from Subcase~(iiia).
Indeed, consider the tree $T^{\prime}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(T)\to a_{\alpha}\bigr].$
Next, consider the tree $T^{5}=T^{\prime}\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{a_{s+t}}(T^{\prime})\to 1\bigr]$,
obtained by attaching all the descendants of $a_{s+t}$ to the leaf $1$ in $T^{\prime}$.
Let $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{s}}(T^{5})=a_{s}\vee\mathcal{F}_{a_{s}}(T^{5})$ be the rooted subtree consisting of the vertex $a_{s}=n-\ell+1$ together with all of its descendants in $T^{5}$.
Finally, we construct the tree $T^{6}$ from $T^{5}$ by removing the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{s}}(T^{5})$ and attaching it to the root $r$ in $T^{5}$.
Then $T^{6}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, and reversing the above constructions, we recover $T$ from $T^{6}$.
Hence $\Phi_{p}(T^{6})=T$, showing that $T$ arises from Subcase~(iiia).
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0ptSuppose there exist integers $s\geq 1$ and $t\geq 0$ with $s+t<\alpha$ such that $a_{s}=n-\ell+1$, $a_{s+j}\in F_{p+2}$ for $1\leq j\leq t$, and $b:=a_{s+t+1}\in F_{\ell}\setminus F_{p+2}$.
Assume further that the vertex $1$ is a descendant of $a_{s}=n-\ell+1$ but is not a descendant of $b$ in $T$.
Then $T$ arises from Subcase~(iiib).
Indeed, the tree $T^{6}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$ constructed above satisfies the hypotheses of Subcase~(iiib), and reversing the construction yields $\Phi_{p}(T^{6})=T$.
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0ptFinally, suppose there exists an index $s$ with $1<s\leq\alpha$ such that $a_{s}=n-\ell+1$, and let $t\geq 0$ be as defined above. Assume that $s+t<\alpha$ and set $b:=a_{s+t+1}\in F_{\ell}\setminus F_{p+2}$.
Suppose further that the vertex $1$ is a descendant of $a_{s+t}$ in $T$.
Then $T$ arises from Subcase~(iiic).
Indeed, consider the rooted tree $T^{\prime}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(T)\to a_{\alpha}\bigr]$.
Let $\mathcal{F}_{(r)}(T^{\prime})$ denote the rooted forest consisting of all descendants of the root $r$, excluding the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{1}}(T^{\prime})=a_{1}\vee\mathcal{F}_{a_{1}}(T^{\prime})$.
Next, obtain the tree $T^{7}$ from $T^{\prime}$ by removing $\mathcal{F}_{(r)}(T^{\prime})$ from $T^{\prime}$ and attaching it to $1$.
We then construct the rooted tree $T^{8}$ from $T^{7}$ by removing the rooted forest $\mathcal{F}_{a_{s+t}}(T^{7})$ and attaching it to the root $r$ in $T^{7}$.
Subsequently, we construct the rooted tree $T^{9}$ from $T^{8}$ by removing the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{s}}(T^{8})=a_{s}\vee\mathcal{F}_{a_{s}}(T^{8})$, consisting of $a_{s}=n-\ell+1$ together with all its descendants in $T^{8}$, and attaching it to the root $r$ in $T^{8}$.
Finally, we construct the rooted tree $T^{10}$ from $T^{9}$ by removing the rooted subtree $\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{a_{1}}(T^{9})$ and attaching it to the vertex $1$ in $T^{9}$.
Then $T^{10}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, and $T^{10}$ satisfies the hypotheses of Subcase~(iiic).
Reversing the above constructions, we recover $T$ from $T^{10}$, and hence $\Phi_{p}(T^{10})=T$.
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\par Since the above cases are mutually exclusive and exhaustive, and every tree $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ arises from a unique element of $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ or $\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, the map
$\Phi_{p}\colon\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}\coprod\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$
is a bijection.
This completes the proof.
\qed\end@proof
\par To determine the cardinality of $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$, we compare the sizes of the subsets $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ and $\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}$.
Our approach is based on Pr\"{u}fer codes of rooted trees, which allow us to relate $|\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|$ to $|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|$, and $|\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}|$ to $|\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}|$.
\par\par We recall that for a rooted tree $T_{0}$ with vertex set $[n]$, where $n\geq 2$, the \emph{Pr\"{u}fer code} $\mathrm{Pr}(T_{0})=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\ldots,\lambda_{n-1})$ is obtained by recursively removing the smallest non-root leaf and recording its parent in the remaining tree.
The final entry $\lambda_{n-1}$ is the root of $T_{0}$.
Moreover, every Pr\"{u}fer code uniquely determines a rooted tree with vertex set $[n]$.
\par\par\begin{proposition}For $0\leq p<\ell-2$, we have
$$|\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|=|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|\left(\frac{n-\ell-1}{n-\ell}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=|\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}|\left(\frac{n-\ell-1}{n-p-3}\right).$$\end{proposition}
\par\par\par\@proof Let $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ or $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, and let $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{\ell-1}(T)$ or $\mathrm{P}_{\max}^{p+2}(T)$, respectively, denote the maximum path of $T$ of the form
$r=n-p\to a_{1}\to a_{2}\to\cdots\to a_{\alpha}$.
Consider the tree $T^{*}=T\bigl[\mathcal{F}_{1}(T)\to a_{\alpha}\bigr]$, obtained by attaching the descendants of $1$ to the leaf $a_{\alpha}$ in $T$.
By construction, vertex $1$ is a leaf of $T^{*}$.
Let
$$\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}=\{T^{*}:T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,p+2}^{p}=\{T^{*}:T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}\}.$$
By Proposition~\ref{prop:T*_in_Cnlp}, the map $T\mapsto T^{*}$ is a bijection from $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ to $\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ and from $\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}$ to $\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,p+2}^{p}$.
Thus,
$$|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|=|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=|\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,p+2}^{p}|.$$
Let $\mathrm{Pr}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,\ell-1}^{p})=\{\mathrm{Pr}(T^{*}):T^{*}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}\}$ and $\mathrm{Pr}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,p+2}^{p})=\{\mathrm{Pr}(T^{*}):T^{*}\in\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,p+2}^{p}\}$ denote the corresponding sets of Pr\"{u}fer codes.
Since the map $T^{*}\mapsto\mathrm{Pr}(T^{*})$ is a bijection between rooted trees with vertex set $[n]$ and their Pr\"{u}fer codes, we have
$$|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|=|\mathrm{Pr}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,\ell-1}^{p})|\qquad\text{and}\qquad|\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=|\mathrm{Pr}(\widehat{\mathcal{C}}_{n,p+2}^{p})|.$$
Let $A=\{2,3,\ldots,n-\ell+1\}$, $A^{\prime}=A\setminus\{n-\ell+1\}$, and $B=\{2,3,\ldots,n-p-2\}$.
\par\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{Enumeration of $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$.}
For every $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$, we have $\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\in A$, so the
first entry of $\mathrm{Pr}(T^{*})=(q_{1}(T^{*}),\ldots,q_{n-1}(T^{*}))$ satisfies
$q_{1}(T^{*})=\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\in A$.
Let $\pi_{1}(\mathrm{Pr}(T^{*}))=q_{1}(T^{*})$ denote the first projection map, and define
$$\varphi_{1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}\longrightarrow A,\quad\text{by}\quad\varphi_{1}(T)=\pi_{1}(\mathrm{Pr}(T^{*}))=q_{1}(T^{*}).$$
Since $\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)$ ranges over all of $A$, the map $\varphi_{1}$ is surjective, with fibers
$$\varphi_{1}^{-1}(a)=\{T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}:\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)=a\}.$$
For any $a\in A$, let $T^{\prime}$
be the tree obtained from $T$ by interchanging the labels $2$ and $a$.
Then $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$ if and only if $T^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}$.
Hence, the map $T\longmapsto T^{\prime}$ is a bijection between the fibers $\varphi_{1}^{-1}(2)$ and $\varphi_{1}^{-1}(a)$.
Therefore, $|\varphi_{1}^{-1}(2)|=|\varphi_{1}^{-1}(a)|$, for any $a\in A$.
Hence, all fibers of $\varphi_{1}$ have equal cardinality.
Therefore, we have
\begin{equation}|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|=\sum_{a\in A}|\varphi_{1}^{-1}(a)|=\sum_{a\in A}|\varphi_{1}^{-1}(2)|=(n-\ell)\,|\varphi_{1}^{-1}(2)|.\end{equation}
Since we can express $\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}=\{T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}:\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\in A^{\prime}\}$, we have
\begin{equation}|\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|=\sum_{a\in A^{\prime}}|\varphi_{1}^{-1}(a)|=(n-\ell-1)\,|\varphi_{1}^{-1}(2)|.\end{equation}
By combining \eqref{eq:|Cnl-1p|} and \eqref{eq:|Tnl-1p|} we obtain
$$|\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|=\left(\frac{n-\ell-1}{n-\ell}\right)|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|.$$
\par\vskip 6.0pt plus 2.0pt minus 2.0pt\noindent{Enumeration of $\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}$.}
For every $T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}$, we have $\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\in B$, so the
same argument applies with the surjective map
$$\psi_{1}\colon\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}\longrightarrow B,\quad\text{given by}\quad\psi_{1}(T)=\pi_{1}(\mathrm{Pr}(T^{*}))=q_{1}(T^{*}).$$
Again, all fibers of $\psi_{1}$ have equal cardinality.
In particular, $|\psi_{1}^{-1}(2)|=|\psi_{1}^{-1}(b)|$, for any $b\in B$.
Thus,
$$|\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=\sum_{b\in B}|\psi_{1}^{-1}(b)|=\sum_{b\in B}|\psi_{1}^{-1}(2)|=(n-p-3)\,|\psi_{1}^{-1}(2)|.$$
Since $\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}=\{T\in\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}:\operatorname{par}_{T}(1)\in A^{\prime}\}$, we obtain
$$|\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=\sum_{a\in A^{\prime}}|\psi_{1}^{-1}(a)|=(n-\ell-1)\,|\psi_{1}^{-1}(2)|.$$
Therefore,
$$|\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=\left(\frac{n-\ell-1}{n-p-3}\right)|\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}|.$$
This completes the proof.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par Combining the results established so far, we can now determine the cardinality of the set $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
\par\par\begin{theorem}For $0\leq p\leq\ell-2$, we have
$$|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}|=(\ell-p-1)(n-1)^{n-4-p}(n-2)^{p}(n-\ell-1).$$\end{theorem}
\par\par\@proof We prove the theorem by induction on $\ell$, for fixed $p$, where $\ell\geq p+2$.
The base case is $\ell=p+2$, equivalently $p=\ell-2$.
By Proposition~\ref{prop:Tnl_bijection}, we have $|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{\ell-2}|=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-2)^{\ell-2}(n-\ell-1)$, which
agrees with the required formula upon substituting $p=\ell-2$.
\par Now we assume that $\ell>p+2$, and suppose the theorem holds for all $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell^{\prime}}^{p}$ with $\ell^{\prime}<\ell$.
Thus by the induction hypothesis, we have
$$|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|=(\ell-p-2)~(n-1)^{n-4-p}~(n-2)^{p}~(n-\ell).$$
Moreover, since $p+2<\ell$, by the induction hypothesis we have
$$|\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=(n-1)^{n-4-p}(n-2)^{p}(n-p-3).$$
By Proposition~\ref{prop:T_vs_C}, we obtain
$$|\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|=|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|\left(\frac{n-\ell-1}{n-\ell}\right)=(\ell-p-2)(n-1)^{n-4-p}(n-2)^{p}(n-\ell-1),$$
and
$$|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=|\mathcal{T}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=|\mathcal{C}_{n,p+2}^{p}|\left(\frac{n-\ell-1}{n-p-3}\right)=(n-1)^{n-4-p}(n-2)^{p}(n-\ell-1).$$
Finally, by Proposition~\ref{prop:Phi_p}, the map $\Phi_{p}:\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}\coprod\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$ is a bijection, and therefore
$$|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}|=|\mathcal{T}_{n,\ell-1}^{p}|+|\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}_{n,p+2}^{p}|=(\ell-p-1)(n-1)^{n-4-p}(n-2)^{p}(n-\ell-1).$$
This completes the induction and hence the proof.
\qed\end@proof
\par Since $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}=\coprod_{p=0}^{\ell-2}\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$, the following corollary is immediate from Theorem~\ref{thm:Cnlp_count}.
\par\begin{corollary}We have
$$|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}|=\sum_{p=0}^{\ell-2}(\ell-p-1)(n-1)^{\,n-4-p}(n-2)^{p}(n-\ell-1).$$\end{corollary}
\par\par We have already shown in Theorem~\ref{thm:|Un|} that $|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-2)^{\ell}(n-\ell-1)$.
Moreover, by Theorems~\ref{thm:SPFGl_to_UnFl} and~\ref{thm:Im(Psi)}, we have
$$|\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})|=|\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})|=|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}\setminus\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}|.$$
One of the interesting applications of the preceding computations is the following explicit enumeration of spherical $G_{\ell}$-parking functions.
\par\begin{theorem}For $n\geq 3$ and $2\leq\ell\leq n-2$,
$$|\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})|=(n-1)^{n-3}(n-\ell-1)^{2}.$$\end{theorem}
\par We first establish the following identity.
\par\begin{lemma}For $n\geq\ell+3$ and $\ell\geq 2$, we have
$$(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-2)^{\ell}(n-\ell-1)-(n-1)^{n-3}(n-\ell-1)^{2}=\sum_{p=0}^{\ell-2}(\ell-p-1)(n-1)^{n-4-p}(n-2)^{p}(n-\ell-1).$$\end{lemma}
\par\par\par\@proof The identity is established by a direct computation. Starting from
the left-hand side, we compute
\begin{equation*}\begin{split}&\hskip-19.91684pt(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-2)^{\ell}(n-\ell-1)-(n-1)^{n-3}(n-\ell-1)^{2}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-\ell-1)\Bigl[(n-2)^{\ell}-(n-1)^{\ell-1}(n-\ell-1)\Bigr]\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-\ell-1)\Bigl[(n-2)^{\ell}-(n-1)^{\ell}+\ell(n-1)^{\ell-1}\Bigr]\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-\ell-1)\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}\Bigl[(n-1)^{\ell-1}-(n-2)^{j}(n-1)^{\ell-1-j}\Bigr]\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-\ell-1)\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{\ell-1-j}\Bigl[(n-1)^{j}-(n-2)^{j}\Bigr]\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-\ell-1)\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{\ell-1-j}\Biggl[\sum_{p=0}^{j-1}(n-1)^{j-1-p}(n-2)^{p}\Biggr]\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-\ell-1)\Biggl(\sum_{p=0}^{\ell-2}\sum_{j=p+1}^{\ell-1}(n-1)^{\ell-2-p}(n-2)^{p}\Biggr)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-\ell-1)\Biggl(\sum_{p=0}^{\ell-2}(\ell-p-1)(n-1)^{\ell-2-p}(n-2)^{p}\Biggr)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt=\sum_{p=0}^{\ell-2}(\ell-p-1)(n-1)^{n-4-p}(n-2)^{p}(n-\ell-1).\end{split}\end{equation*}
This proves the lemma.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par Combining Theorem~\ref{thm:|Un|}, Corollary~\ref{cor:Cnl_count}, and
Lemma~\ref{lem:key_identity}, we obtain the following corollary.
\par\begin{corollary}We have
\begin{equation*}\begin{split}|\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})|&=|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|-|\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}|\\
&=(n-1)^{\,n-\ell-2}(n-2)^{\,\ell}(n-\ell-1)-\sum_{p=0}^{\ell-2}(\ell-p-1)(n-1)^{\,n-4-p}(n-2)^{p}(n-\ell-1)\\
&=(n-1)^{n-3}(n-\ell-1)^{2}.\end{split}\end{equation*}\end{corollary}
\par\par\begin{remark}Lemma~\ref{lem:key_identity} admits a natural combinatorial interpretation.
The identity reflects the decomposition
$$\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}=\Bigl(\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})\Bigr)\coprod\left(\coprod_{p=0}^{\ell-2}\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}\right),$$that is, every tree in $\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}$ belongs either to $\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})$ or to exactly one of the subsets $\mathcal{C}_{n,\ell}^{p}$.
Rearranging the identity of Lemma~\ref{lem:key_identity}, we also obtain a decomposition of the elements of $\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})$ according to the choice of root in the corresponding trees.
\end{remark}
\par\par We are now in a position to prove Theorem~\ref{thm:|SPF|}.
\par\@proof[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm:|SPF|}]
By Theorem~\ref{thm:SPFGl_to_UnFl}, the spherical $G_{\ell}$-parking
functions are in bijection with $\operatorname{Im}(\phi_{G_{\ell}})$. Hence, by
Theorem~\ref{thm:Im(Psi)} and Corollary~\ref{cor:image_count},
we obtain the stated formula.
\qed\end@proof
\par\par\begin{remark}An \emph{$\ell$-claw} is a graph with $\ell+1$ vertices consisting of a central vertex adjacent to $\ell$ other vertices, with no edges among the outer vertices.
Equivalently, it is the complete bipartite graph $K_{1,\ell}$.
The graph $G_{\ell}$ is obtained from $K_{n+1}$ by deleting an $\ell$-claw at vertex $1$, that is, by removing the $\ell$ edges joining vertex $1$ to the vertices of $F_{\ell}$.
More generally, let $\widetilde{G}_{\ell}$ be a graph with vertex set $[n]\cup\{0\}$ and root $0$, obtained from $K_{n+1}$ by deleting an $\ell$-claw at some vertex $i\in[n]\setminus\{1\}$,
that is, by removing the $\ell$ edges joining $i$ to distinct
vertices $j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell}\in[n]\setminus\{i\}$.
By relabeling the vertices, we may assume $i=1$ and
$\{j_{1},\ldots,j_{\ell}\}=F_{\ell}$, so $\widetilde{G}_{\ell}$ is isomorphic to $G_{\ell}$.
Hence their skeleton ideals are isomorphic, and in particular their $(n-2)$-skeleton ideals have the same number of standard monomials. Therefore,
$$|\mathrm{sPF}(\widetilde{G}_{\ell})|=|\mathrm{sPF}(G_{\ell})|.$$Thus, Theorem~\ref{thm:|SPF|} computes the number of spherical
$G$-parking functions for every graph $G$ obtained from $K_{n+1}$ by
deleting an $\ell$-claw.
\end{remark}
\par\par\begin{remark}Although the previous remark shows that every graph obtained from $K_{n+1}$ by deleting an $\ell$-claw has the same number of spherical $G$-parking functions, a combinatorial description of spherical parking functions in terms of uprooted spanning is not known in general.
For such a graph $\widetilde{G}_{\ell}$, the image of the map $\phi_{\widetilde{G}_{\ell}}:\mathrm{sPF}(\widetilde{G}_{\ell})\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}(\widetilde{G}_{\ell}^{\prime})$, where $\widetilde{G}_{\ell}^{\prime}=\widetilde{G}_{\ell}-\{0\}$, need not admit a characterization analogous to that obtained for $G_{\ell}$,
and the associated uprooted spanning trees may be considerably more difficult to describe.
For example, let us consider $\widetilde{G}=K_{n+1}-\{e_{n-1,n}\}$.
For $n\geq 3$, the map $\phi_{\widetilde{G}}:\mathrm{sPF}(\widetilde{G})\longrightarrow\mathcal{U}(\widetilde{G}-\{0\})$ is injective but not surjective.
In particular, when $n=4$, $|\mathrm{sPF}(K_{5}-\{e_{3,4}\})|=12$, whereas the number of uprooted trees with vertex set $[4]$ having no edge between $3$ and $4$ is $17$.
\end{remark}
\par\par The results obtained in this paper suggest several natural directions for further investigation.
While we have obtained explicit enumeration formulas and combinatorial descriptions for spherical $G_{\ell}$-parking functions and uprooted spanning trees, many questions remain open.
We conclude with some problems and directions for future research.
\par\noindent{Question 1.}
\emph{The formula for $|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|$ in Theorem~\ref{thm:|Un|} is given by
the simple product
$$|\mathcal{U}_{n}^{1\not\sim F_{\ell}}|=(n-1)^{n-\ell-2}(n-2)^{\ell}(n-\ell-1).$$
Does there exist a direct combinatorial proof of this formula,
perhaps via a Pr\"{u}fer-type encoding or a bijection analogous
to that of Chauve, Dulucq, and Guibert~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CDG}{}{}]}?}
\par\par\noindent{Question 2.}
\emph{Determining $|\mathrm{sPF}(G)|$ for an arbitrary graph $G$ appears to be difficult, and explicit formulas are known only in a few cases.
Can such formulas be obtained for other natural families of graphs, such as complete bipartite graphs?
In this direction, Kumar, Lather, and Sonica~\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{CGS}{}{}]} established that for $m\geq 1$,
$$|\mathrm{sPF}(K_{m+1,2})|=(m-1)2^{m}+1.$$
It would be interesting to determine $|\mathrm{sPF}(K_{m,n})|$ for general
$m$ and $n$.}
